madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the greatest theft in history, as defined by the united nations, continues unpunished before our eyes: even today, women worldwide earn on average 23 percent less than men. europe is not doing enough: it is not enough to give women representative shares in the boards of large companies, representative shares which are not at all sufficient to solve the problem. it is an illiberal solution: the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work is a welcome one. the wrong and dangerous message that women are a "protected" category with pink quotas is really risking to weaken, rather than strengthen, the contractual strength of women. on the contrary, the proper recognition of skills and merit will be the only possible solution to combine equal opportunities with equal dignity.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, wage discrimination is one of the most widespread and aberrant forms of social inequality, which prevents women from fully participating in the economic and social growth of their country. this discrimination has a lot to do with the cultures and stereotypes that characterize today's social patterns and labour market dynamics at national levels. commissioner dalli, your proposal for pay transparency is acceptable, but as the five star movement we ask you that the new proposal for a directive not be just another list of statements of principle and good intentions, but that it should provide the member states with solutions to solve once and for all the problem of gender segregation in the various professions and sectors of activity, which is the main cause of the pay gap between men and women. it is also necessary to start discussing a harmonised system of sanctions, which would punish those who flagrantly violate the right to equal pay and those who deliberately exploit women's work to the detriment of justice and social cohesion.
madam president, when europe needs to renew its unity and look beyond the horizon, maintaining the gender pay gap is not only unjust to women, but also anachronistic. it insults women as full—fledged members of society. when the brightest women earn less than their male colleagues, it also reduces europe’s chances in global competition. to make our labour market fit for the 21st century, we must get rid of the famous 16% differential. also, the fact that today women are better educated does not mean that in the future the labour market will be more woman friendly. girls are not encouraged by families, schools, universities or businesses to learn skills in new technologies. the pay gap can grow in the future. as a gender stereotype, the pay gap is a cost to the entire society. that is why i am convinced that this house will spare no effort to support the commission action to ensure that the gender pay gap will be abolished entirely.
madam president, i would like to say to the commissioner that to close the gender pay gap is not a symbol, but a way to restore social justice in our democracies. not only is there the pay gap, but women are also victims of glass ceilings since they are not often promoted at the top level of their career. women are victims of family duties and tasks since they are often the only caregiver in the house. women are victims of gender bias and often not considered adequate enough. women are also victims of violence, and more than 30% have experienced one form of violence in their lives. so this is the real fight, a fight for decency, for justice and for change. we have to fight for a day on which women will be fully equal to men, on which they will be fully aware of their value and their potential, and on which no more women will have to choose between a child and a job. so please let us close the pay gap as soon as possible within the next three months. you have to promise us: ‘you’re not alone, we are with you and we will never give up’.

mr president, the points made by ms melchior and others were absolutely right. the comitology system is opaque and nobody in the wider public understands it. but of course we do have another system for delegating powers to the commission, which is delegated acts, where it is clear and simple. we delegate powers to the commission, but we can block their decision if we don’t like it, and we can even retrieve the powers that we have delegated if we wish to do so. this can be done unilaterally as parliament, even though it was a joint decision with the council to delegate the powers. is that not an argument to make much greater use of delegated powers rather than implementing acts? mr szájer and i were the co-rapporteurs on the delimitation between implementing acts and delegated acts, but, frankly, we should make much more use of delegated acts. they are transparent and democratic.

author. – madam president, on 17 september 2019, mr adam farkas announced his resignation from his position as executive director of the european banking authority (eba), which will be effective on 31 january 2020 to become, starting on 1 february 2020, the ceo of the association for financial markets in europe (afme), a well-known lobbying company that, according to its website, is the voice of europe’s wholesale financial services. let me remind you what the eba’s responsibilities are: the eba is responsible for supervising the eu’s banking sector and for setting technical standards implementing key financial regulations. this makes the eba a fundamental institution that regulates and supervises the financial sector. it also, of course, makes it a constant lobbying target for the industry. afme, like other stakeholders, appears to have permanent interactions with the eba. in compliance with the staff regulations and ethical rules, the eba’s board of supervisors has taken a decision to impose fairly light restrictions on its executive director, both while in service at the eba and after leaving the eba. however, the restrictions on mr farkas’s future employment do not seem either realistic or enforceable. unless mr farkas simply becomes afme’s poster boy, i cannot really find a single topic that afme covers that does not fall within the remit of the eba and does not relate to the work of mr farkas. moreover, it is not clear to me who will monitor the effective application of these restrictions, in particular the one that provides that he cannot engage in lobbying or advocacy of the eba or have professional contacts with eba staff for 24 months after leaving the authority. how are we going to enforce that or control that? who’s going to do that? so, for this reason, we call for a review of this decision by the eba board of supervisors. sadly, mr farkas’ is not the first case, and will probably not be the last, of a post—public employment job in the private sector that poses evident problems of conflicts of interest, but we absolutely must act to ensure that this is the last. i really hope that this is the last. revolving doors, where one jumps from public institutions to top positions in the private sector, from regulators to regulated, or from supervisors to supervised, and vice versa unfortunately not only undermine the credibility of the individual engaging in such behaviour, but undermine the credibility of the whole institution and, more generally, the trust that citizens have in public bodies, and the trust that citizens have in public institutions is the backbone of democracies. so we shall not tolerate our european institutions and agencies’ credibility crumbling just because of some individuals’ interest, and we should always remain vigilant in making sure and guaranteeing that they are not influenced by private interest. for this reason, we call on the commission to assess the current practice on post—public employment at european and national level, to extend this review to pre-public employment conflicts of interest, and also to put in place a harmonised legal framework, which should include an extension of the possibility to block professional moves and the provision of a cooling—off period that is proportionate to the specific case and accompanied by an appropriate temporary allowance. finally, we call on the commission also to assess whether it is appropriate that the eu agencies concerned decide for themselves on the enforcement of the rules for the prevention of conflicts of interest and how a consistent application of the rules can be ensured. i was glad to see that the resolution was supported unanimously by members in the committee on economic and monetary affairs (econ) with no votes against and no abstentions. i hope this is a clear signal and that the plenary widely supports the resolution as well.

on behalf of the ecr group. – mr president, madame lagarde, i think price stability was and remains the primary objective of the ecb. you cannot have price stability without financial stability; financial stability is a precondition for price stability. now what do we see in the world and in europe today? extreme low interest rates, strongly-reduced credit spreads, softened credit terms, feverish mergers and acquisition activity, high levels of speculative ipos, spectacular growth of shadow banking, equity valuations totally out of line with historic price-earnings ratios, and usd 15 trillion of negative-yielding debt. i think it’s fair to say, if you see those eight developments all together, that financial stability is under a lot of pressure – and that’s an understatement. unconventional monetary policies have been contributing to these developments and so should really be, basically, fundamentally reconsidered.

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, every seven years we see this game of cards, where on the one hand there are the pragmatic, the realistic, those who have to make up their minds, and on the other hand there are some tobacco salesmen who pretend to be ambitious. but these days, it's realism that counts. this morning, frankly, i heard little of realism. i've heard good news from the night of a thousand and one nights. i have heard rumours of a budget that would reduce inequality, pollution, unemployment - a long and expensive wish list. but those who have the capacity to administer the public good start thinking about waste and inefficiency first. for years now, we have been stressing the need to give priority to the most effective budgetary policies, from enterprise to agriculture, research and innovation. but when it comes to cutting funds for the external action service, for example, which is a useless duplication of national embassies, or cutting contributions to turkey, for which the european union has become the atm, this house suddenly turns a deaf ear.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, at the time of 5g, some areas in the french periphery still have no access to the digital world. what has the european union done? in these and many other areas it is far behind and its present functioning does not allow it to serve the nations. the european union is a global community of nations, and its citizens are the most important citizens in the world. the european union is operating on a basis of free trade which is becoming less and less fair and less and less equal. the social europe we want is a coordination between the european nations and equality between the territories. we do not want the globalism advocated at this assembly, which concentrates all its activities in a few cities, we do not want it, we do not want it any more. we oppose your ultra-liberalism and fair trade. we oppose your urban concentration to the local proximity. all this in a democratic and modern europe, which relies on digital technology to make people and nations shine, failing to submit to the european union and its commission.
madam president, today we can recognize the importance of digital technologies in our lives, the impact on our productive and social model. states will have to take important decisions to prepare for this transition. digitalization cannot lead to dehumanization. we are facing a new model of work. we should prevent automation from causing the disappearance of a large number of jobs. the training of young people and workers must be adapted to new occupations with agility and flexibility. we must ensure that workers are properly and effectively protected against these new challenges, while at the same time promoting the competitiveness of our enterprises, their investment and their growth. we have a great opportunity to revitalise rural and depopulated areas by giving their inhabitants access to digitalisation, to achieve greater economic, social and territorial cohesion.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, first of all we must ensure that no one is left behind in the opportunities offered by the digital revolution. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the quality of the information it receives, and has also set up a number of working groups to examine the impact of the digital economy on the quality of life of citizens. we have created oligopolies which in the european tradition of free competition must be regulated, ensuring that the digital giants pay their taxes and avoiding excessive concentrations and a type of unfair competition, i am thinking especially of digital trade in relation to the traditional economy. the commission has also proposed that the in the coming years, we should be able to use digital to better protect health, to implement agricultural production and to certify the origin and quality of our products and to fight fake news, because it's true that a lie on the web can travel faster, but it can also be unmasked just as quickly, and we have a duty to create structures and systems that can do this in a fast, continuous and effective way.
madam president, technology is changing the way we work, study and train. it is also creating new types of jobs and connectivity gives us new opportunities, but it also means new challenges for us. the work-life balance directive that i successfully negotiated in the last legislature addresses all these developments. it will make it easier for women to enter and stay in the workforce to reduce unemployment as well as to reduce the severe gender pay and pension gap we have. i welcome the commission's commitment to making the strategy, because we are facing psychological challenges in relation to the use of mobile phones and laptops. the right to disconnect should be part of this process. for these new risks european workers must be protected and we must have new safeguards. and we must be at the forefront of ensuring that rights are accessible to all.

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the internet in general has gone from being a simple communication infrastructure to a mass phenomenon of sociological interest. we are living in an era of digital transformation that is changing our habits, undeniably simplifying our daily lives but hiding some aspects that if not constantly monitored can have negative repercussions. the ease with which any type of content can be shared and accessed is a very powerful weapon for those who want to take advantage of situations that are not regulated and are easily manipulated. we must therefore commit ourselves to constant monitoring of all those technological applications, as well as of digital content, which could endanger individual freedom. it is therefore vital that the legal system should provide clear rules guaranteeing the freedom of the citizen, so that it is not sacrificed by mere artificial rules.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, we support the importance of gender equality as a common value. if we are to be consistent, we are working to promote it in all the policies of the european union, as well as in parliament, but as a cultural revolution and not as a tax act. barriers to women's participation in the workforce have particularly high costs for well-being and growth. removing obstacles is important. research by the european institute for gender equality shows that gender equality would promote economic growth. but does the institute also monetize the role of women in the reconciliation of family and work? improvements in gender equality would create over ten million additional job opportunities in europe and gdp per capita would grow by almost 10% by 2050. but what balance for families? gender equality will also have to be promoted in the context of the new eu policies digital single market, green deal and artificial intelligence in which the employment gap is even more dramatic. now a new commissioner with an exclusive mandate on equal opportunities will have to change the management of previous equal opportunities policies, also because the european court of auditors will examine the integration of the gender dimension in the eu budget and, according to what has been made public today, the audit report will be released in spring 2021. we shall then see what effect the policies of the von der leyen commission have on the world of women.

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the european union and its member states have been engaged in the fight for gender equality for years, but the results to date show that the policies put in place have not been sufficient to overcome the economic, social and cultural damage generated by this discrimination against women. the reason is very simple, because the economic and productive world has never wanted to intervene in a convinced way in the workplace where discrimination is systematically and daily applied. we therefore welcome this gender equality strategy, provided it is innovative in relation to the major changes taking place, such as the industrial and digital revolution, which, if not managed well, will surely increase the disparities between men and women. we must therefore devote to this strategy all the resources and policies we can to end all forms of discrimination against women in the workplace and in their retention in the labour market. as european institutions, together with civil society, we must do everything we can to put an end to this physical and psychological violence against women once and for all. commissioner dalli, if the strategy is up to all these challenges, we will support it and together we will win these battles which are about civilization, but above all about social justice.

in the name of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i thank mrs metsola for her work. in recent years we have seen a marked increase in document fraud in the schengen area, both through the falsification of genuine documents and the use of counterfeit documents. this measure will allow us to frame the management of the system for online forged and authentic documents, known as fado, within frontex, whose management model is now obsolete. the need for protection and security that our citizens demand of us requires us to ensure a very high level of alert against false documents, as criminal instruments suitable for reuse for various crimes, including money laundering and terrorism, but above all illegal immigration. in fact, forged documents have often been used by asylum seekers trying to enter europe. these are behaviours which we cannot tolerate and which must be stopped. it is therefore essential to provide the police, border guards and customs with a more efficient database, capable of allowing for the rapid identification of falsified documents and an effective fight against these phenomena. for these reasons we support the proposal, despite our expressed disagreement with the fact that some private parties have access to fado, even if only to a limited extent. although we are against the reform of the frontex agency regulation, we believe that the importance of fado in the anti-fraud activities carried out by the authorities of the member states is now overwhelming.
mr. president, forging documents is a proven method for criminals and terrorists to stay under the radar. we see that frontex is already detecting some 7 000 cases a year and that number has only increased in the schengen area in recent times. this is a matter of concern, for where false documents are used, our security is at stake. it is therefore crucial that police and border guards throughout europe can check the authenticity of documents from other member states, that they are aware of the technical specifications of those documents from other countries, and that counterfeits circulating in the eu are really widely known. there is a real european added value in this: sharing information between member states, ensuring that that information is always up to date and ensuring that that information is accessible and available to all 24/7. this new system is therefore a very welcome new system and very necessary, because in the field of security too we are stronger together than alone. i congratulate roberta metsola on the excellent result, together with her colleagues, and i look forward to seeing the system actually working in practice.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the fight against false documents and their circulation in the schengen area is now a priority for the european union in the context of cooperation between law enforcement authorities and in the fight against criminal organizations. however, effective enforcement requires a constant exchange of information, not only between the authorities of the eu countries but also, with due regard for personal data, with the european institutions, third countries and private entities such as airlines, which can contribute to the proper functioning of the fado system. in fact, numerous investigations by europol have repeatedly shown that false documents are often the means used by criminal organisations to commit other, much more serious crimes, such as human trafficking, migrant smuggling or even preparing terrorist attacks. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this crime. the commission is therefore pleased that the regulation transfers the responsibility for the system for handling false and authentic documents from the council to frontex, thereby completing the legislative reform of the agency, which will, however, have to be followed by a proper and complete implementation in order to make the rules so far approved really effective. i therefore thank mrs metsola for her work and i hope that this measure will be supported by the widest possible consensus in this parliament.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the agreement we are voting on is technical in nature, but there are at least two political aspects i should like to stress. firstly, document fraud is a serious threat to us all and the cooperation between the member states which is inherent in the fado system is a clear success, one of those cases where the concept of 'more europe' is being declined in a functional rather than instrumental way. the second policy element, which is unfortunately much less positive, can be summed up in the definition of 'data minimisation'. the restrictions introduced for the retrieval of personal data represent a missed opportunity to effectively counter organised crime and irregular immigration. the left in this parliament has once again shown its disconnection from reality.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the farm-to-table strategy is widely shared by both farmers and consumers, especially in my own country, italy. on the other hand, it is not an economically neutral strategy. the right choice of sustainable agriculture weighs on the cost of production and consequently on the purchase price of products. i am thinking in particular of organic farming and the fight against intensive farming. that is why, without adequate economic support, without a cap that protects small and mediumsized farmers against large companies, we risk only environmental demagogy and nothing more. food labelling will also be decisive. the european union must not regard it as optional to inform consumers correctly about the geographical origins and nutritional properties of food. good work, commissioner.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i am addressing this house in a situation which is not the usual one, of course. europe is going through a difficult time, but we must continue to work and we must do so also for those who are on the border between turkey and greece and for those who have been trapped for months, sometimes even years, on the greek islands. it is the selfishness of our member states that has caused all this suffering, that has led children to self-harm and even suicide. we need immediate measures of solidarity with those who are trapped there. we do not need to defend our borders but we need to establish an emergency relocation mechanism, first of all for unaccompanied minors. it's unthinkable to leave children in such conditions! we must be close to greece, of course! but we must also demand clearly and unambiguously that the greek government restore full respect for human rights within its borders, because the images of the past few days have made me ashamed as a human being first and as a european second.

in writing. – i support this proposal because we need to respond as soon as possible to this immense and unprecedented economic, social and political challenge called coronavirus. the coronavirus epidemic sweeping across europe has made one thing abundantly clear: you can’t fight an epidemic alone. it requires urgent, decisive, and comprehensive action at the eu, national, regional and local levels. the european economy and single market have become so essential to our well-being yet we only recognise the value of them when it comes to a halt. with thousands of airplanes grounded, factories all across europe shutting, and millions of businesses suffering, our european interconnectedness – or rather the temporary lack of it – has become painfully visible. therefore, this exceptional situation needs to be addressed with specific measures to support and protect member states’ economies, companies and workers. i fully support the eu commission ‘coronavirus response investment initiative’ which aim is to mobilise more than eur 37 billion quickly in order to address the consequences of the coronavirus crisis. it is a matter for the whole eu and we need common action and solidarity if we want to respond quickly and effectively to covid-19 crisis.
in writing. five hundred million europeans are being subjected to the shock of covid-19, the magnitude of which is unprecedented in our personal memory. two things are certain: (a) the catastrophic consequences of the crisis are still dependent on the european institutions and their capacity to respond; (b) everything is in question - our daily lives, our security, our behaviour - but also the usefulness and even the raison d'être of the eu as a space governed by law, a community of values and a globally relevant area of cohesion and solidarity. the eu will be wrong to underestimate the risk to its reputation and credibility imposed by this crisis. the european council of 26 march could only take decisions to match the enormity of this call, namely: a) a liquidity guarantee to administrations and companies at zero rates; b) suspension of the rigors and release of the fiscal capacities to ensure that the victims receive all the aid they deserve. the european union is also supporting the european investment bank, the european central bank and eurobonds to combat the virus and to rebuild what is being damaged. if the treaties set limits, it is time for extraordinary solutions. if the instrument is the esm, no conditionality or memorandum of understanding is acceptable under any circumstances.
in writing. we are living in an extraordinary situation that challenges us to take exceptional and unique measures and that needs strong leadership. helping each other is helping yourself. we should have learned this from all the crises we have overcome before and which have shown that only together can we overcome adversity. the choices we make today will define our future. we are facing a public health emergency, and the union will do everything in its power to save lives. the measures approved today will directly help all the professionals who work miracles every day with the resources at their disposal. many countries, like mine, are in a state of disruption and the emergency mechanisms we are putting in place here today, with 90% of the union's funding, will allow for effective and rapid aid. i also welcome the response of our scientists, who, united in a group led by the european commission, are constantly looking for a vaccine or more effective means of dealing with this pandemic. we will overcome this situation together, and the solidarity we show in our actions today will bear fruit and tomorrow we will be more united in this unique project which is the european union.
in writing. – today, we are voting on three measures - the start of the eu’s response to the pandemic. but we can do more. european solidarity will be essential. the eu can play a vital role to maintain supply chains for medical equipment and food. we heard terrible stories from colleagues from italy and spain, the worst affected countries. we must stand with our partners. finally, we need to consider if the eu is fully equipped to deal with global crises like this. on animal health, the eu has stronger powers which means there is more coordinated eu action on disease outbreaks like african swine fever. this isn’t the case for human health. our strength on animal disease was only developed after the bse crisis. the covid-19 pandemic is a crisis we cannot afford to waste. it requires us to acknowledge the need for more coordinated eu action on health - more europe, not less. i want a europe that acts before a crisis hits, a europe well-prepared to handle future crises, a europe that leads globally and helps locally. we can do it. perhaps it is this shock to the system which will make us act - better late than never.
in writing. – while some may argue that the debt or the migration crises, we faced in the past decade, required only eu solidarity to cope with, as not all member states were equally affected, the current covid-19 humanitarian crisis requires unprecedented decisiveness and scale of the eu response. for the eu, which began 2020 with expectations for an economic recession by the end of the year, the pandemics couldn’t have come at a more inconvenient moment. this means that we must adopt radical measures to mitigate the most severe negative effects of the covid-19 crisis. saving human lives and keeping eu businesses alive are top priorities at the moment. the financial injection for member states we are about to vote today is an adequate measure to support member states’ budgets with additional funds. i am also a strong supporter of the ecb and the eib group actions to ensure enough liquidity for the eu financial system. besides, i believe a decisive measure with an imminent and direct effect would be the eu to loosen the short-term austerity restriction such as the fiscal compact requirements so member states could deploy large scale fiscal measures timely.
in writing. the european union and the world are now facing a crisis of a completely unprecedented nature. the covid-19 pandemic is not only a health crisis, but also an economic crisis. the european commission proposes to redirect €37 billion to disaster management, allowing member states to strengthen their health systems. the european union, as a model of solidarity, therefore wishes to provide adequate support to the challenges facing the member states in these times of crisis. however, the way in which european financial assistance is used will be left to the governments of the member states. it is the task of the european institutions, in addition to providing aid, to ensure that the leaders of the countries allocate the money they receive, because human lives are at stake, and the stakes are high. the global challenge must be met with european unity. i would therefore ask my fellow members to vote in favour of the proposals put forward by the commission to ensure that europe can respond as quickly as possible to this health and economic emergency.
in writing. we'll vote for these proposals, but it's far too little. it's not business as usual, it's not a situation like any other and we can't solve it again in a bureaucratic manner. european solidarity must not be just a concept in a treaty. the funds allocated at european level are far too little compared to the real needs. but where do we get the funds if some states don't understand the need to contribute more to do more things together for the citizens we represent. we need a common european database of all people in quarantine, home isolation or suspected coronavirus, and it needs to be accessible to healthcare staff and those who are on the ground every day. we need medical and protective equipment, masks and medicines in sufficient quantities and affordable for everyone and all member states. we need to allocate all resources to resolve the medical crisis and prevent its return. we need to work together to identify the antidote and vaccine as soon as possible. we need to quickly regain confidence in democratic institutions and the economy by identifying effective recovery measures, new, fairer formulas and the allocation of sufficient resources.
in writing. europe is facing the biggest crisis in recent times, affecting public health and the eu economy. we can only tackle this global problem together, and the eu must act decisively to bring the pandemic under control. member states should be provided with adequate medical supplies, best practices should be actively shared to combat the coronavirus and cooperation between countries should be established to ensure the smooth functioning of trade. it should also be understood that, although the current health crisis is serious and international law allows for the use of emergency powers in the event of major risks, all measures in the coronavirus response must be proportionate, necessary and non-discriminatory. finally, countries where the spread of the virus is slowing down should seek to normalize life and avoid a situation where emergency powers are unreasonably prolonged.
in writing. – the coronavirus outbreak represents a challenge to the eu. coordinated and urgent responses are necessary to alleviate the effects of this sanitary crisis. the aim is to mobilise cohesion funds and redirect them towards the most urgent needs: support for healthcare, social temporary measures for the workers and employees as well as enterprises, in particular smes. the unused pre-financing (€8 billion) received by the member states in 2019 won’t be called back by the commission and can be reoriented to address this crisis. the proposal would enable disbursement of the structural funds through this existing liquidity of €8 bn. (together with eu co-financing of €29 billion would bring €37 billion support from the eu budget). the mss can also use funds, which are not contracted to finance acquisition of medical assets, equipment and materials, to promote technical unemployment schemes and finance working capital for enterprises etc. we call upon the ec to promote additional simplification measures to allow the use of all available funds, including increased co-financing rates (95%), transfer of funds among categories of regions and avoid decommitment risk in 2020. solidarity and responsibility across our societies and between member states will be key to overcome this challenge.
in writing. the image that europe is offering to our citizens, not only worried by the dramatic health crisis but, in many cases, literally crouched by the economic damage caused by the epidemic containment measures, is the frame of an administrative elephant trapped in the swamp of bureaucracy, and the representation of a supranational organization politically disoriented. the selfishness of some member states is paralysing the need to make clear, in practice, that the crisis due to covid-19 affects every single country. only a joint assumption of responsibility, by sharing the debt arising from the emergency, could help to restore, albeit in a limited way, a semblance of solidarity at community level. if the health emergency continues to be exploited opportunistically and immorally by the 'usual well-known' in order to impose their national economic and financial agendas, we will see a final and justified abandonment of the european project.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the european union's own resources, which make up the budget, are actually provided by the member states by means of vat or gross national income. the new resources that are being brought to the table will be confronted with the vetoes of the powerful lobbies in europe. the ecb, unlike other foreign central banks, cannot finance the european budget directly, nor are there any other forms of own resources that do not result in additional taxes for european citizens. the next mff, the instrument on which european solidarity should be based, will therefore only be a game of chance for some and a net loss of resources for others. the countries with very wide spending margins are already financing their own economies, leaving no room for any mutualisation of sovereign debts or eurobonds. the instruments put in place, including the recovery fund, will therefore only be able to provide loans by forcing already highly indebted countries to borrow even more and by placing the burden of this crisis on the innocent citizens, the only lenders of last resort, since the treaties forbid european central banks from playing this role. but if those same citizens are unable to work today because of the pandemic, who will produce the income necessary to repay that debt? the european union cannot continue to shout slogans without producing results. if the treaties are not to be changed by radical reforms, including in relation to the mandate of the ecb and the budget, words such as 'ambitious targets' or 'no one will be left behind' will remain mere slogans and the union will not achieve any objective, even a modest one, because many of its citizens have already been left behind.

vice-president of the commission. – mr president, i would like to thank you for this very important discussion. we are speaking here about the emergency regime in hungary and i think that it’s very important that the commission is monitoring the emergency measures taken in hungary, but also in all the other member states. we live in very challenging times, we live in an extraordinary situation which requires extraordinary and special measures to be taken. when we look at the extraordinary measures and the emergency regimes, we have to – and i’ve also repeated what i said at the beginning – look into how the regime is applied, and here there are very clear requirements that measures must be – only to the extent necessary – relevant for the solution or tackling the corona crisis, proportionate measures and also time a limit. and the time limit is something where we have the biggest concerns regarding the hungarian regime. the commission is monitoring the situation in all states, but in the case of hungary i can reveal to you today that i have daily reports. i have weekly reports from our permanent representations, but i am following very closely the hungarian situation daily reports. i am very well informed about the cases now of two people who were detained in relation to the spread of so-called fake news. i am informed about the actions taken by the government in relation to self-government and so on. on a daily basis we are assessing whether we can take legal action. and i said before, and i will repeat it here: we are not opening infringement proceedings on hungary yet. we are closely monitoring all the measures, and we also have to take into consideration that in our toolbox is also article 7 and that the debate in the council is ongoing and i am sure we will come back to it when the council holds the general affairs council session. but on concrete measures, because we said that we are monitoring all the concrete measures taken under the emergency regime, i would like to be more concrete here to give you some examples. the commission’s attention has been drawn to certain degrees which allow employers and employees to agree bilaterally to derogate from almost all provisions of the hungarian labour code. as the labour code transposes several eu directives the commission is currently analysing these measures as regards their compatibility with eu labour and social law. also, the commission is examining several decrees as regards their compatibility with the general data protection regulation. just these two examples of the concrete focus of the commission in these days. and indeed, many of you mentioned here that there should be eu money should be provisional on the distribution conditioned by the basic principles of the rule of law in the countries. well, when i came with this idea in 2017, it created a lot of noise, people said ‘we cannot go that far’. i think now, with the situation developing in a wrong direction in a couple of states, we see that making money conditional on compliance with the basic principles set out in article 2 of the treaty is the proper thing to do. today, the commission will have the debate about the multiannual financial framework, about the budget for the next seven years. i will emphasise again and again in every fora at which i have a chance to speak about the new budget, that the conditionality has to be maintained and that the system of voting about the concrete cases must not be voted down. and i am happy that this is still in the negotiating box of the budget and it has to remain there and it has to keep its teeth because the commission proposed something which might really create or bring about some real difference. we have now the joint progressively replaced by more targeted interventions in line with constitutional arrangements of each country. so this is what we expect hungary to do, to go along with the rest of the other member country which is under the article 7 procedure. so we must build our new steps and next steps on the basis of common values. we must come back to normal checks and balances and the citizens must presidency recognises the crucial role that rule of law plays in all our democracies. it is a key guarantee that our common values are protected and a joint responsibility of member states and all eu institutions. this is no less important when facing the challenges of the covid-19 pandemic where unprecedented circumstances led many member states to adopt far-reaching measures in order to rapidly and effectively curb the spread of the virus. member states have already given broad support to the commission’s initiative to monitor the extraordinary measures and their application across the union on an equal basis for all member states. similar efforts, based on equality of approach and monitoring developments across the union, are underway by different european institutions, the fundamental rights agency, but also the council of europe. furthermore, the commission is in the process of preparing the first annual report on the rule of law in the union, to be published later this year, focusing on several rule of law areas. during the croatian presidency, this process remains in the preparatory phase. the commission is leading the work and the member states are involved in all the process. i mentioned earlier that article 7 procedures concerning poland and hungary were on the agenda of the general affairs council on 25 march. however, due to the covid-19 emergency, that meeting had to be cancelled. unfortunately, the emergency is not over and for the time being, it is not possible to hold a formal council meeting. as we already mentioned, on 6 and 22 april, emergency measures adopted in response to the covid-19 crisis were discussed at informal video meetings by ministers of justice and european affairs. the ministers exchanged views on national and eu measures taken so far in response to the covid-19 outbreak, including their duration, scope and effectiveness, as well as the impact of emergency measures on fundamental rights and the rule of law. ministers shared their experience and best practices in the overall context of a collective eu response. the council remains seized of the article 7 procedures and the croatian presidency is ready to continue proceeding at the general affairs council as soon as the situation allows us to convene formal council meetings. we continue to follow all developments closely. at the same time, we hope that the ongoing dialogue at the political level between the commission and the member states concerned will bring some tangible results. the presidency will pay particular attention to the views expressed here today during this debate.

in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, on the coronavirus health emergency, europe is making serious mistakes. there was a lack of solidarity between european countries (you will all remember the failure to send respirators and masks to the most affected countries, such as italy), each country acted differently on tampons and the count of deaths and then also on treatment, medicines and vaccines: europe has been totally absent. but today i want to talk to you about the hyperimmune plasma treatment, a simple treatment that has been around in medicine for almost a century, now being tested on covid-19 in mantua and pavia, italy, with great results. i'll give you a little bit of data. in the case of very serious patients in intensive care, mortality is reduced from 15 to 6%, and in mantua, for those who are not very serious, mortality is reduced to zero. think about it, 116 universities in the united states are already doing it. let's talk about the costs. antiviral drugs can cost four, five, six times more than a simple bag of plasma. yet neither the italian government nor you in europe seem particularly interested in the matter. why would you do that? well, it doesn't have big pharmaceutical companies behind it, it doesn't have big investment and big power in the industry, it doesn't have big contracts and millionaire profits. maybe some people are better off going somewhere else. this is a mistake. we are asking that, pending a vaccine, europe should financially support this therapy and encourage the establishment of dedicated plasma banks in the various european countries.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the pandemic has shown us in recent weeks that the european labour market is much more integrated than we imagine. we have understood that without the free movement of workers, especially seasonal and cross-border workers, entire productive sectors which are strategic for the european union risk being blocked. the worker, from the moment he leaves his country of origin via the green corridors, needs to be protected by high standards of protection, housing, working conditions and remuneration, until full recognition of his acquired rights in terms of social security, which for us must fall to the country where the worker performs his work and where he pays the taxes. we believe that the union must promote the harmonization of national labour laws in order to solve wage dumping, but also to deal with the new dumping which is taking place and which is based precisely on the health and safety of workers. i think we can support this resolution, especially if it can be the basis for new legislation to better protect european workers and to eliminate that unfair competition which unfortunately still characterizes the internal market ... mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i must say that i agree, albeit with some reservations, with the content of the resolution that has been tabled on the protection of crossborder workers, a category of workers particularly affected by the covid-19 crisis. as far as italy is concerned, over one hundred thousand workers cross the border every day to work in other countries, such as france, austria and even san marino. of these, however, more than 70 000 work in switzerland. this is why, on the one hand, the call in this resolution must be extended to neighbouring countries, such as switzerland or the united kingdom, to ensure decent working conditions and adequate protection measures, in order to protect all frontier workers, particularly seasonal workers who, by the nature of their contractual basis, fall into a particularly vulnerable group. on the other hand, we hope that the states will implement in concrete terms, and not just in words, the protective measures for those frontier and seasonal workers who have lost their jobs as a result of the covid-19 crisis. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we are well aware that the measures taken by the member states to contain and combat the spread of the virus have had very serious consequences for crossborder and seasonal workers in the states where they are employed. i think about how they found themselves immediately and suddenly without income, housing, transportation, health care. i would like to say a special word to the seasonal workers in agriculture, who are often victims of the corporal tax. a plague, unfortunately, very common in the regions of my southern italy. this resolution is a clear and acceptable response to the serious problems facing these categories of workers. i hope, therefore, that the commission and the states will ensure full protection of rights, by sanctioning any discrimination against workers, and that the council will guarantee the necessary resources to boost employment and provide protection and support for workers and firms that have been penalised.

vice-president of the commission. – madam president, i would like to thank very much all the honourable members for taking the floor. i was quite extensive in my introductory remarks, so now i can be much briefer. i would just like to react to the four most frequent issues which have been highlighted by the honourable members. the first is the sense of urgency mentioned by mr mureşan, by mr lamberts and by mr andresen, who made reference to ‘zeit, zeit, zeit’, and also to mr tajani, who highlighted how important it is to send a strong signal now. i think that this should be the absolute priority for our leaders. i think we cannot wait any longer. we need to get a deal at the next european council because that would be the best signal, the best response we can provide, first to our citizens. it would be the best response to the crisis. it would help us to secure jobs, and i think it would be a very important sign to our global partners, financial markets and representatives of industry that europe is back, that we are overcoming the crisis, we are introducing new policies and will be emerging from the crisis stronger than when we entered it. a second very important group of remarks referred to the social aspects, to the thrust of the policies to make sure that we would underpin the green and digital transformations together with resilience. there have been some question marks on how all this would be organised, so i just want to reassure all honourable members that the recovery plans would be drafted by the member states themselves. of course we want to make sure that the issues, the accumulated problems which have been building up in all member states year by year would be tackled. gradually we would be removing them and by that we would really be achieving the resilience which should be stronger in our member states and for the whole european union. the commission will, of course, be in a close iteration process with our member states and we are ready to inform the european parliament, as we always do, under the consultations, under the structured dialogue, under the hearings, when it comes to the european semester process. the european parliament will have strong oversight over all expenditure as it does right now when we are reviewing expenditure on an annual basis. this was also, i would say, the thrust of the remarks by ms aubry, mr pieper, and also by ms hohlmeier, to make sure that this process is transparent and that the role of the european parliament is properly maintained. as ms marques suggested, i think right now we have the first meeting of our leaders and presidents under article 324 and, as i said in my introductory remarks, we are ready to make a proposal for institutional declarations which would really put in a very clear framework how we see our close cooperation among the institutions on the important parts of implementation of next generation eu and the mff in the future. the last point, which was raised by ms virkkunen, who was our last speaker, but also by mr benifei, ms hayer and mr cormand, was on own resources. indeed this is a very important question, because we have to propose new ways of how we are going to pay, with money which would be raised on financial markets, to finance our resilience and recovery instrument. we are considering, as was highlighted, the emission trading system; also the carbon adjustment mechanism, but also an own resource based on operations of enterprises in the european single market and the digital tax. we continue to work on our plastics tax and on some other ideas which are coming along in this process of deliberations and discussions. what we want to propose to you is that we are ready to work on the roadmap, on how we want to assess, when we want to propose, and how we want to debate these potential new resources because we know that it’s important for this parliament. it’s also very important for the national parliaments, because as you know, if we want to increase the ceilings of our resources and financing, we need to get the support of all eu member states’ national parliaments. so to conclude, i really would like to thank you for your support, for the sense of urgency, for your readiness to cooperate in the very dynamic way which would be needed for the next semester. i’m very much looking forward to our debate. once again, be assured that the role of the european parliament will be properly maintained.

the next item on the agenda is the debate on the statement by the council of the commission on a comprehensive union policy on the prevention of money laundering and terrorist financing commission action plan and other current developments (2020/2686 ((rsp)). i would point out to the honourable members that there are no spontaneous exchanges of words or no blue cards in this debate.
the president of the council. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. this is a first for me today, a very special moment. i have been a member of parliament for 22 years and a minister of state for europe for seven years, but i have never been able to speak before the european parliament. i'm not doing this ad personam, i'm doing this for the council. i am very grateful for this opportunity and i look forward to the next few months. i am always at your disposal and i look forward to working with you in a constructive and critical way in these difficult times. the fight against money laundering and terrorist financing is one of the key issues of the german presidency of the council. it is also a subject which, as we know, the council as a whole is very committed to. the european parliament, the council and the european commission have worked closely together in recent years to create an effective system for combating money laundering and terrorist financing. we have made considerable progress here, not least in the legislative field, and this is of course due in particular to your commitment, ladies and gentlemen. i would just like to mention the amendment to the directive on combating money laundering, the banking package, the regulation on the european banking authority and the new legislation on criminal law and the use of financial data for law enforcement purposes. but, ladies and gentlemen, mr president, that is really no reason to rest on any laurels, we are still being reminded all too often that there are still alleged cases of money laundering involving european banks. this must be unacceptable to all of us! in the meantime, there has been a wide-ranging political debate within and between the institutions of the european union on how to make our framework for combating money laundering and terrorist financing even more effective in the future: what to do, where do we simply need to improve? the commission's proposals are a good example of this, but they cannot be confined to the present situation. i would like to mention a few examples which also show how we have been involved. in december 2018, the council agreed on a concrete action plan to combat money laundering, the implementation of which we continue to monitor. in december 2019, the council adopted strategic conclusions, in which it invited the commission, inter alia, to explore ways and means of achieving greater harmonisation in the area of anti-money laundering. the question is to examine the advantages and disadvantages of a supervisory body at european level. the most recent example of our commitment is the council conclusions on improving financial investigations, which were adopted on 17 june - a few weeks ago. i am not saying that we should not be concerned about the future of the community. we agree that we need to make substantial progress. on 7 may, the commission presented its latest package of measures to combat money laundering. it contains the commission's action plan and, in particular, very important legislative proposals to be presented in the first quarter of 2021. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we intend in particular to make a thorough examination of the commission's action plan and to provide the commission with further guidance in the form of council conclusions. we hope that these conclusions will help the commission in the preparation of the legislative proposals it intends to present next year, so that we have a system to combat money laundering and terrorist financing that has the greatest possible impact. there's really air up there. i have the impression, ladies and gentlemen, that in the preparations for this item of the agenda, you, the members of this house, the whole of the european parliament, the commission and the council are equally aware of the urgency. moreover, i wish all of us the utmost political ambition to tackle the great problem of money laundering in the european union even more effectively than we have done so far!
executive vice-president of the commission. – mr president, thank you for this opportunity to present the commission’s approach to fighting money laundering and terrorist financing. we have made a clear commitment to deliver a comprehensive approach to fighting dirty money, to better protect our economy and financial system in the future. this is one of our top priorities and i know that they can count on your full support. the commission fully shares the european parliament’s view that it is high time to create an effective enforcement policy in this field, where all the pieces fall together and where the roles of the private sector, and the competent authorities in the member states and in the eu are clearly set out. the commission adopted an action plan on 7 may on a comprehensive policy to prevent money laundering and terrorist financing. this ambitious and multifaceted plan sets out measures that the commission will undertake over the next 12 months to better enforce, supervise and coordinate the eu’s rules on combating money laundering and terrorist financing. our aim is to shut down any remaining loopholes and remove any weak links in the existing eu rules. any flow of money of dubious origin, any company created without a real business behind it, any offer from a suspicious investors to buy property – these all create opportunities for criminals and we must crack down on each and every one of them, wherever they are in europe. our first and most critical objective is to make sure that everyone in both the private and public sectors applies the relevant rules rigorously. the commission is doing its bit. we have launched numerous infringement procedures to ensure full transposition and application of our two core directives, the fourth anti-money laundering directive (amld 4) and amld 5. since i took office in december, the commission has referred three member states to the court of justice for non—transposition of the fourth anti—money laundering directive. we have sent out three reasoned opinions for the incomplete transposition of the fourth directive, issued four letters of formal notice for non-conformity in the transposition of the fourth directive and 17 others for non—communication of measures under the fifth anti-money laundering directive. we have also closed nine cases for non—communication under the fourth anti-money laundering directive, recognising the important efforts undertaken in some member states. aml rules must be effective. that is why commission proposed in may to issue country—specific recommendations to 11 member states in this area. the european banking authority now has additional powers and i expect it to use them in full. in terms of the next steps, i would like an eu aml supervisory body to conduct systematic on—site checks in member states. we need to build up our preventive capacities. but we also need to go further and strengthen our rules and tackle remaining weaknesses. let me highlight some key issues. cases of online money laundering are on the rise. authorities in all member states should have the powers to freeze suspicious transactions of this kind. however, we are also aware that the way rules are currently implemented is not optimal for legitimate businesses. clearer and risk—based rules will avoid companies facing unexpected consequences due to divergences in the way rules are applied. our rules should not make life harder for honest operators. honourable members, the commission fully concurs with you – national supervisors cannot supervise rules on their own. this has become abundantly clear from the string of scandals we have recently witnessed. dirty money is highly mobile. if it is dealt with in one member state, it moves to another one with weaker supervision and enforcement. dirty money should have nowhere to hide. this is why we will have to establish eu—level supervision. an eu supervisory body would require direct supervisory powers over the riskiest financial institutions, with oversight for the non—financial sector. different options for eu supervision are possible, as set out in our consultation. we may propose a new eu agency or add to the responsibilities of the european banking authority, depending on the feedback we get and on our own assessment. however, if the european banking authority is to become the eu supervisor, both its track record and governance will have to improve. let’s be clear, an eu supervisor cannot effectively enforce aml rules in the eu unless additional flanking measures are in place, and they are critically needed in three directions. first, our work towards better preventing and fighting money laundering will be supported by the european public prosecutor’s office, europol and eurojust, but also through better protection of whistle—blowers with a comprehensive new regime that member states will need to transpose by the end of next year. second, we will set up a mechanism to improve how suspicious flows of money are tackled and reported and better coordinate this analysis across member states. we know that too much money laundering takes place across borders. third, aml does not stop at the eu borders, so strong rules and enforcement in third countries is also essential. not all countries, however, have strong regimes in place. the new methodology issued alongside the action plan provides the eu with the necessary tools to take action. in this respect, i commend the positions taken by both the european parliament and the council with respect to the delegated act on the list of high—risk third countries, which recently entered into force. to conclude, to effectively fight money laundering, we need to act in multiple directions. let me assure you that the commission is fully ready to play its part to ensure that our internal market and financial system are protected from criminals. the commission thanks the european parliament for steering as this debate towards a successful conclusion.
on behalf of the ppe group. other money laundering is crucial for criminal networks to make illicit profits. as a union we need to be stronger in the fight against all this. it is not enough to have paper money laundering laws, they need to be enforced. criminal banks are often allowed to continue to operate despite clear evidence that they are facilitating money laundering. and accountants' companies, such as nexia bt, are allowed to continue in a parallel world where there are no laws to stop them. the commission has also been asked to provide information on the implementation of the eu's anti-corruption strategy and to provide information on the implementation of the eu's anti-corruption strategy. in malta, daphne caruana galizia was killed because the institutions failed to do their job. we need to rise to the occasion and ensure that no person is left alone in the middle between criminals and their illegal profits.
