author. – madam president, with this motion for a resolution, the european parliament calls on the commission to proceed with the chemicals strategy for sustainability. chemicals are everywhere. they are in our food, in our water, in our clothes and in our cosmetics. they are in the air that we breathe. in other words, they affect all aspects of our daily life. our first priority is to upgrade the level of protection of the consumer and the environment. for this reason, we are asking for the elimination of risk-hazardous substances and substitutions to safer alternatives when it’s feasible. we call on the commission to take into account the risk posed by endocrine disrupters and the combination effect of different chemicals. commissioner, we are focusing on a very ambitious target by aiming to set the chemical safety standard for the entire world. the european chemicals agency (echa) should support the eu’s international trade policy by defining provisions and fostering regulatory systems built upon eu standards for chemical safety. this would enhance protection beyond the eu borders and extend the level playing field. chemicals are also the industry of industries. a size—based grouping approach will enable the industry, in complementarity with the ‘one substance, one assessment’ principle. this strategy must also grant sufficient time for the industry’s manufacturers in order to deliver safe and performing products and incentivise the eu industrial production and economy. in this regard, the strategy should secure adequate funding to accelerate the development of innovative and safe alternatives. the strategy should also ensure that unnecessary animal tests are avoided, incentivising the development of smart testing strategies and artificial intelligence methods. however, everything is not dependent on this house. everything is not dependent on brussels and on brussels’ political will. that’s why we also have to increase the level of implementation of the existing eu legislation on chemicals, asking the member states to fully implement the current legislative framework in order to provide a safe and sustainable environment as well as a competitive european market. in this strategy, we have three critical allies – the three agencies: echa, the european food safety authority (efsa) and the european medicines agency (ema). firstly we must avoid overlap between these three agencies. secondly, we have to secure their adequate funding by providing additional resources. i would like to conclude with the need for the chemicals strategy for sustainability to be aligned with the two main aspects of the green deal. the first one is green and the second is digital. in this regard, we fully support echa’s initiative to promote the establishment of a fully-connected and interoperable eu chemical safety database platform to facilitate the seamless sharing of data between authorities and to provide public access to researchers, regulators, industry and the citizens at large. κυρίες και κύριοι συνάδελφοι, επίτροπε, κυρία πρόεδρε, η στρατηγική για τα βιώσιμα χημικά μπορεί να οδηγήσει τη γενιά μας και τα παιδιά μας σε ένα μέλλον με λιγότερα προβλήματα. μπορεί να οδηγήσει τη βιομηχανία σε βιώσιμες λύσεις με πολύ χαμηλότερες επιπτώσεις στο περιβάλλον. αρκεί τα χημικά προϊόντα που εισάγονται από τρίτες χώρες στην ευρωπαϊκή ένωση να έχουν ακριβώς τις ίδιες προδιαγραφές με αυτά που παρασκευάζονται και καταναλώνονται στην ευρωπαϊκή ένωση. η αγορά είναι παγκόσμια και το στοίχημα για τα βιώσιμα χημικά αφορά τον πλανήτη, αφορά τη ζωή μας.
member of the commission. – madam president, today’s debate and the resolution you will adopt tomorrow on the upcoming chemical strategy for sustainability come very timely and i would like to thank you for the opportunity to reply today to the issues you raise in your oral question and to give you a brief update on where we are with this strategy. as announced in the commission’s work programme for 2020 – we have updated recently to respond to the corona pandemic – the chemical strategy is planned for the third quarter and we are currently working towards an adoption at the end of the third quarter this year. internal discussions and preparatory work are still ongoing, so i’m sure you will understand that the information i can provide today remains general and that i cannot yet go into any details of the upcoming strategy. the chemical strategy for sustainability is one of the key deliverables of the green deal and one of the priorities, of course, within my portfolio this year. when we adopted the green deal last december we all agreed to ensure a toxic-free environment. the strategy had a twofold objective. first, it should help to protect citizens and the environment better against hazardous chemicals and it had to encourage innovation for the development of safe and sustainable alternatives. this twofold objective has guided our internal preparatory work from the very beginning and has also made it clear from the very start that to better combine health and environmental protection, and increase global competitiveness, all parties and stakeholders have to work closely together. this also applies, of course, to the commission, which is why my services, my team and i are preparing the strategy in a very close cooperation with many other dgs, cabinets and commissioners involved in this very horizontal, i would say, topic. i appreciate that in the resolution that you will adopt tomorrow you equally acknowledge this horizontal character of chemicals. you highlight both the need to act towards a safe and sustainable use of chemicals, but also the essential role of the chemical sector in reaching the multiple targets of the green deal, such as the zero-pollution ambition, climate neutrality, energy transition, the promotion of energy efficiency and the circular economy. let me already thank you for the considerable work and effort you have put into this important resolution which, as i said, comes very timely. once adopted, we will examine it very carefully and take it into account when finalising our chemical strategy over the next weeks. we will, of course, also take into account the over 400 comments and contributions we received from stakeholders and citizens on the roadmap for the chemical strategy, which was published for feedback from 9 may until 20 june of this year. let me now provide you with some more elements on our upcoming chemical strategy for sustainability. the strategy is based and will build on a number of policy evaluations and previous initiatives linked to the eu chemicals legislation and in particular the second reach review from 2018, the fitness check of chemicals legislation from 2019, the communication on endocrine disrupters from 2018 and the communication on options to address the interface between chemical, product and waste legislation from 2018. to reach our main objective, the strategy will particularly focus on the following five elements. first: strengthening legislation for higher health and environmental protection from hazardous chemicals. despite the great achievements of eu chemicals legislation, we urgently need to step up action, in particular to better protect vulnerable groups, to address the combined effects of chemicals, to tackle endocrine disrupters and persistent substances. second: boosting innovation and competitiveness. we need to incentivise the development of safe and sustainable production and use of chemicals. the transition to ‘safe and sustainable by design’ represents a major opportunity for the eu’s innovation and competitiveness. at the same time, a strong and sustainable eu industry is an important asset for the eu to contribute to global sustainability. we also need to support the recovery from the economic crisis, build resilience and ensure strategic autonomy in the supply of essential chemicals in europe, for instance for health applications or technologies critical to achieve climate neutrality, digital transition, clean and safe material cycles and zero pollution. third: streamlining the existing framework. our legislative framework is overall fit for purpose, but also complex, and in some cases inconsistent and slow. this is a challenge for both stakeholders and authorities. we have the opportunity to rationalise and simplify and make it more effective, including by moving towards a ‘one substance – one assessment’ process. insufficient enforcement and poor compliance are also major bottlenecks, preventing existing legislation from attaining its full potential. fourth: intensifying research and knowledge on the impacts of chemicals on health and the environment, but also ways to better process and share this data and information for regulatory use, but also for consumer information or industrial use in the supply chain. one key area is the tracking of hazardous substances in articles to achieve non-toxic material cycles and ultimately a circular economy. finally, strengthening the global dimension. it is important that the eu becomes more and more an example worldwide of standards on sound management of chemicals to strengthen international standards and support third countries in the safe production and use of chemicals. once the final chemical strategy for sustainability has been adopted by the college, i will, of course, be happy to come back and present it to you and i already look forward to working very closely with you to implement it, because the work will, of course, not finish with the adoption of the strategy, quite the contrary. for now, let me thank you for your attention and i look forward to the views of the political groups.
madam president, in its communication on the green deal for europe, the commission announced its ambition for a 'zero pollution' environment free of toxic substances. in this context, reducing exposure to hazardous chemicals is of course a major issue and the body of legislation regulating chemicals must be a bulwark for citizens and the environment against the major risk posed by some of these substances. through our resolution, parliament is sending a clear and ambitious message, firstly for full implementation of existing legislation, but also for the development of legislation in many areas. strong measures for health and the environment are needed and we must take responsibility for them. we want health to be put before the economic interests of certain industries. what is sustainable chemistry? it is necessary to adopt precise criteria to define it, otherwise sustainable chemistry will be just a concept that industries will use to 'greenwash' their activities, to the detriment of the health of citizens. a more sustainable chemistry is not one that generates more and more income for the shareholders of the chemical industry, but one that fully integrates the objectives of public health and biodiversity protection. this is achieved primarily through the reduction of hazards from chemicals, by replacing the most dangerous substances with safe alternatives, and through increased transparency of the authorisation process, which also takes into account the social and environmental costs, particularly for persistent chemicals. the cost of the chemical industry to health today is €160 billion a year. if these costs were borne by industry, i can assure you that the substitution with alternative substances would go much faster. so that is where we have to go.
on behalf of the renew group. madam president, commissioner, our resolution sets a course for you today: the course for the sustainable strategy for chemicals, which the commission will therefore present to you before the end of the year, with an objective which we obviously share, namely to strengthen the protection of the health of citizens and the environment, an ambitious objective: 71% of chemicals manufactured in the european union are classified as dangerous. so the commission - because we have had some leaks - wants to strengthen the regulatory framework, that is good news. it is, of course, aimed at reach in particular, with a grouped approach for similar substances in particular to speed up the risk assessment by our european chemicals agency. one small thing in this respect: this logic by family must also apply to the restriction procedure for dangerous substances, phthalates, to name just one example, banned in children's toys, allowed elsewhere. we are told that the commission is also considering new criteria for risk management. european legislation must of course protect against endocrine disruptors, against neurotoxic substances. i am thinking here of the many workers who are exposed to solvents or heavy metals on a daily basis. finally, it is essential to act against pfas, these perfluorinated compounds found in foams, teflon pans - not to mention food packaging. pfas, because they're mobile, because they're persistent, very persistent, they're a real danger to our health. the european union must move forward, commissioner, with the phasing out of this class of almost 5 000 chemicals. i would also like to note, and conclude here, that last december the dutch government put the question, the debate, to the council. so the window of opportunity exists, and we must seize it.
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i actually see several criticisms in this strategy, starting with the overly ideological approach. if we want concrete results for the environment, we cannot demonize a sector such as chemistry - an excellence at european and italian level and above all a sector already advanced in terms of sustainability - by imposing additional unsustainable burdens. without a pragmatic approach, we risk hitting european chemistry and then finding ourselves importing the same products from outside europe, from where the standards we impose are not guaranteed. it is therefore a short-sighted attitude which is harmful to workers and our businesses. we must not forget the importance of relocating chemical companies and their production to europe, an objective which must not be pursued by imposing fake green taxes, just to make money, but by means of instruments which favour the sector, bringing back to europe the capacities we have left abroad. without pragmatism and balance, we risk only penalizing a strategic sector for the whole of europe, a sector which should instead be actively supported in its transition to even more sustainable production.
the commission is therefore proposing to extend the period of validity of the directive to the period of 1 january 1999. the commission has already done so. the european parliament is calling for nothing less than a paradigm shift in european chemicals policy for our health, a toxic-free environment and the sustainable restructuring of the chemical industry. the european commission must pursue a zero-tolerance strategy towards toxic chemicals in the environment. the commission has just this week published new figures in germany: one in five children - and this is alarming - is highly exposed to chemicals in everyday objects. the burden is so great that long-term damage cannot be ruled out in one in five children. in particular, hormone-altering chemicals such as bisphenol a and co. and perpetual chemicals such as pfas must be banned as a group. but ambitious chemicals policy does not only protect our children. it is also an opportunity for a european industry that is not sleeping in on the future. clean chemistry in europe can not only create a clean environment for people and nature, but also stabilise 1.2 million sustainable chemistry jobs. we are expecting an ambitious strategy from you, commissioner.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i should first like to thank all my colleagues and the shadow rapporteurs for their work on this report, which is intended to highlight the european parliament's recommendations in the run-up to the negotiations for the conclusion of the agreement between the european union and new zealand on the exchange of personal data between europol and the new zealand authorities responsible for combating crime and terrorism. as we all know, terrorism is still a serious threat today and the fight against these criminal acts is a top priority, not only for the european union but for all the member states. we have mourned too many victims in recent years, fellow citizens who have become the casual target of unprecedented hatred and violence, and we owe them our commitment. the inclusion of new zealand among the countries with which to start cooperation follows the christchurch attack of march 2019, also considering that the european union and new zealand are partners that share the same principles, vision and approaches on many aspects of global scope. to date, europol and the new zealand police have signed a working agreement providing a framework for structured cooperation, but a legal basis, which is essential for detecting, preventing and prosecuting terrorist offences, is lacking. in my report i set out a number of objectives which will have to be pursued in the course of the negotiations in order to allow the use and transfer of personal data for security reasons, while ensuring the protection of the fundamental rights of individuals. in this context, i believe that the text which will be put to the vote represents the right combination of the two requirements, reminding us that the fight against terrorism and its international dimension deserve our full attention and our political and legislative commitment, and i hope that the house will give concrete recognition to this tomorrow.
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the identity and democracy group, and in particular the lega-salvini prime minister movement, have often been accused of wanting to destroy the european union. it 's not like that . we do not want to destroy the european union, but to improve it. i myself, on 26 october 2019, sent a letter to president sassoli asking for the constituent process of the european constitution to be resumed. the answer i received from the president confirmed the need and the will to continue in this direction, stressing the centrality of european culture. the european union must be reformed or rather reborn; it must not be a europe of nations but a europe of peoples. we remember the great venetian people, or the friulian, bavarian, catalan, basque, breton and so on people. we should not speak of a european culture, but of a set of cultures and peoples that make it up. the attempt to stifle and eliminate the different specificities can only lead to the inevitable failure of the european project, a failure which we do not want. only full respect for the culture and history of individual countries will lead to a genuine european union. a european culture can only be created by going beyond the national interests that compose it, and individual identities are the added value and make europe great and unique, with almost 500 million inhabitants. this is why european culture must become a priority in the framework of european policies and form the main pillar of the future european constitution. there can be no european union without respect for the rules and individual identities, without respect for the will of the people, even if this is inconvenient for the european high finance and bureaucracy.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, culture cannot be forgotten. the creative and cultural sectors are strategic: we need a new deal for culture, starting from the emergencies of these months, the facts of social distancing, empty cinemas and theatres, production blocked. culture means content but also work, often precarious, and industrial realities. the commission's recent proposal for the next mff does not meet the challenges we are facing: the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very good one. next generation eu can create links with other sources of funding, promote partnerships and synergies. european cultural diversity must be at the heart of the new development model: we need to work to strengthen existing programmes, such as creative europe, and maximise the opportunities of the recovery fund. we are waiting for concrete answers.
in the name of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, when we talk about policies for social cohesion and for the good of the peoples of europe, we cannot help but recall what has just happened and what is still happening in relation to the economic and social crisis caused by the coronavirus. so much talk and proclamation, but so far so little action. it's better to promise little and deliver than to make big announcements. more flexibility is needed and only then will it be possible to bridge the gaps and promote a détente that leads to greater equality. greater investment is needed to close the social gaps in some regions, to protect and promote local specificities and identities, and to foster convergence and cohesion. this is the challenge facing the european union and we shall see if it is able to meet it. i hope that the sad lesson of covid has been learned, otherwise european social cohesion will deteriorate to the point of no return.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, today is a historic day for all european citizens: we are voting for a major change, one we have been waiting for for decades, from the short-sighted policies which europe has pursued, disappointing, penalizing and impoverishing millions of its citizens. as an italian and a european, i thank president giuseppe conte for the excellent result achieved at the european council and my colleagues in the five star movement for the synergy created with him and the valuable work done. next generation eu must mean enough, forever, of the austerity policies that have impoverished the poorest and created de facto discrimination between citizens of different states and different races. we turn the page and we have to turn it all the way. so watch out for the cuts in the programs, which we hope to correct. this shift cannot be limited to the post-covid emergency, but must be the paradigm of the europe that will come: the commission's proposal is to be adopted by the council at the next meeting of the european council. let us therefore commit ourselves to keeping the promises made today, without ever going back, in the spirit of the founding fathers, without whom europe would have no reason to exist.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i thank president michel and president von der leyen for being here today. this is a very important discussion, but without a premise it risks losing its meaning: in the last 20 years, the european institutions have introduced a complex set of rules that are incomprehensible to our citizens, a set of rules that helped make europe, even before the covid emergency, and especially at that time, the slowest growing region in the world. not having a moment of self-criticism on this is a serious problem in my opinion. it is clear, as many have said, that the agreement reached at the european council is macroeconomically significant, as we all know. even the supposed benefits for my country are still not entirely determinable: we do not yet know how we are going to finance these measures, and some of the assumptions we make are worrying because they risk undermining some of the productive sectors which are still important and which need support. on the contrary, we already know what the european commission will recommend to my country: he's gonna ask for cuts to pensions, health care and social services. i do not want us to be here in a few years to say that the pension cuts in italy have financed the production of electric scooters in china. i would not like this veil of hypocrisy, which has been drawn from maastricht to lisbon and continues to this day, to be unveiled at last, because it risks giving the impression to the citizens that the crisis and its gravity have not been understood and that solutions such as these are just a useless panacea to solve the problems.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, over the last 20 years italy has been a net contributor to the european union's coffers of some 160 billion, if we limit ourselves to a simple arithmetical sum and not capitalized and also include direct and indirect contributions to support countries in financial difficulty, including instruments such as the edf and the esma. for those who have really done the math, and not with colored lenses, perhaps blue with outline stars, it is very clear that this time too italy, at the end of the entire complex next generation eu program, including the odious compensations due to the continuation of rebates, will continue to be a net contributor, even at the cost of literally slaughtering fiscally and socially, with primary surpluses, citizens and businesses, who would bring herds of angry rhinoceroses to their knees. i therefore invite those who are talking about the marvellous advantages that italy has achieved at the european council, including those who are now reaping great benefits by becoming net importers of tax residences of companies in the euro area, to do their homework first and then clean their mouths before making unnecessary remarks about my country. president von der leyen, if a country defaults on its debt in these years, who will pay for it the debts contracted under the next generation eu programme?
in the name of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, the tragic experience of the health emergency has made us realise that instruments such as european civil protection must necessarily be strengthened. in fact, it is not possible to forget that at the height of the crisis in italy, and despite the requests of the ambassador, no one - and i stress no country in the european union - responded positively to the request for help. the aid has indeed arrived, but only from small countries such as albania or even from distant cuba. this, ladies and gentlemen, must not happen again. that is why we believe that the rapporteur's proposal, which seeks to strengthen this instrument, must be accepted and is heading in the right direction. i would also like to take this opportunity to thank commissioner lenarčič, who is not here tonight, and his offices, of course, for the great commitment and availability which he has always shown in answering my questions and letters. although the commission has also made european civil protection available to strengthen monitoring at italy's borders along the balkan route, the minister of the interior of my country continues not to ask for it to be used, condemning the citizens of my region, friuli venezia giulia, to continuous waves of migration and then having the border police operate without the appropriate protection instruments. it is a strange thing to hear from a lawyer, but in this case the european commission has tried to meet the needs of my citizens more than the italian government, which, unfortunately, has been totally and culpably absent on the balkan question.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, mr high representative, over the last four years the 28 now 27 member states of the eu have been the world's second largest supplier of military equipment, accounting for 26% of total exports. these are important figures which, on the one hand, demonstrate the competitiveness of the industrial sector, but on the other, call into question the guiding instrument for the granting of export licences, that legally binding common position which is often, almost always, not respected because there are no consequences for infringements. i strongly believe that this process must be accompanied by as many instruments of control, finally community, going beyond the common position. we have done a lot for defence, creating the new european defence fund and talking about military mobility, but this cannot go hand in hand with the introduction of a common export regime, including a monitoring body and a robust sanctioning mechanism for all governments that are violating. we need to do this to be credible, if we really want to be that global actor, that champion of peace and diplomacy that we so often declare.
madam president, mr vice-president, we are discussing today the implementation of the council's common position on arms exports to third countries. if we are honest with ourselves, this common position remains a wish list. a typical example is turkey: in the last two years alone, we have had illegal invasions of syria and an illegal invasion of iraq, the violation of the arms embargo in libya - where turkey even threatened frigates operating in the framework of operation peace - the violations of the sovereign rights of greece and cyprus, and of course a threat of war - the well-known "casus belli" dating back to 1995. the council has already called for the common position on turkey to be strictly applied since last october, but nothing has changed. we therefore ask you, in the amendment we have tabled, to take the initiative to stop european arms exports to turkey. the european arms cannot be used to threaten two of our member states. and what you have to do is the least bit of solidarity, and it has to be done immediately.
madam president, this parliament has repeatedly called for arms exports to saudi arabia to stop. but these calls are ignored. spain has a long tradition of arms deals with saudi arabia and the government of mr sánchez, keeping up with this tradition, has authorised sales for about 400 million in 2019. deals with the saudis are the family business of the spanish royals, a business that operates under the complacent watch of all spanish governments since 1975. king juan carlos’s affairs with his saudi buddies are now finally under investigation, but not in spain – in switzerland. what are the spanish authorities doing about this? nothing. they not only refused to investigate, they participated in the cover-up. they sent the king to abu dhabi to hide away from swiss justice and they keep on selling weapons to the saudis. this must stop. corrupt interest in member states cannot take precedence over our moral obligations.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the trade in and export of arms must be guided not only by industrial logic but above all by european foreign policy strategies and, in this context, by strict respect for human rights and our fundamental values. we must therefore improve transparency and parliamentary control and define a sanctions mechanism to ensure that the rules are fully respected. the situation is very worrying indeed: we have to tell each other. we think of the fact that several member states continue to export weapons and tools used for internal repression to egypt, a country where the absence of human and democratic rights is blamed, and this despite a decision of the european council in 2013, which is blatantly ignored. or the business - let us think about this too - always linked to the arms industry, which too many do in turkey, or what is happening in libya. the control of arms sales is certainly not the solution to all problems but it is an essential element in sending clear and strong political signals which are sorely needed.

in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, we have heard the commissioner say that we need a more coordinated approach on covid: well, it is the middle of september! let's just say the timing is off the mark. the european union has been nonexistent and ineffective. i 'll take a quick picture of you . each country manages the health emergency autonomously; there is no coordination with the european union and often not even with neighbouring countries; the borders are closed without any clear logic. quarantines are imposed differently from one state to another; there's no clarity about when and to whom to tampon; the 15-day quarantine for the positive is now in question. i mean, it was total chaos. you said, commissioner, that we must introduce common lines: you're wide awake! we are in a paradox. i heard earlier from the european council that, you know, they're doing a really hard job with meetings and meetings on covid. too bad they're the same ones who cut 7.7 billion euros from the health program in the middle of the pandemic: the commission's proposal is to be adopted by the end of the year. as for immigration, italy, accomplice of the unworthy government, supports the arrival of thousands of immigrants, many of them positive for covid. the european union does not care. there is no common assistance, no distribution of refugees, no solidarity. in short, the borders are there for everyone except for italy, which is forced to take on new health risks. i conclude: your inability to do so is simply proof that this europe is not working.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, forests and woodlands cover 43% of the union's surface and directly employ more than 500 000 people and indirectly 2.6 million. 60% are privately owned and managed by the member states. the commission's proposal for a regulation on the protection of the environment is a very important one. a new forestry strategy is therefore required, with at least three priorities. first: integrating sustainable forest management and the multifunctional role of forests based on environmental sustainability first and economic and social sustainability second. secondly, to include the forestry strategy as an integral and independent part of the new european green deal. thirdly, to take concrete action to stop both global deforestation and possible new privatisations at the expense of national parks and to encourage not only reforestation but also the sustainable management of forest resources. we therefore believe that the cap can play a crucial role in sustainable and multifunctional management and that funding for forestry measures and the development of the bioeconomy should be increased in rural areas.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, forests are one of the greatest expressions of nature, but in the name of a vicious development we are destroying what we should be preserving for future generations. i think the report approved by the agri committee is innovative, because it states that the forestry strategy is one of the pillars of the new green deal and that it is essential to provide economic and regulatory instruments to protect and expand existing forests. we must then choose to abandon old patterns. we in the five star movement believe that biomass must be eliminated from european and national energy strategies, as must hunting, which can no longer be considered a source of income or a recipient of european funds. we are aware that more than two million workers are in the forestry economy, but that does not necessarily mean that they are exploited in an unbridled and indiscriminate manner. the virtuous role models are the foundation for building a european ecological framework. so, it's a huge challenge and it's counter to what's happening in the rest of the world today. only europe is seriously pursuing this objective, with the active participation of its citizens and its productive and social forces. this is the model to be built for a true green transition.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, we are all aware of climate change, of the need to protect nature, of the urgency of protecting the environment. we're all on the front lines, no matter what. but the european union's approach, let me say, europe has a somewhat ideological approach, it is somewhat subservient to greta's slogans and it has a potentially harmful approach for the continent. yes, i said harmful. because your policies risk bleeding economies to death in the face of results that, frankly, are negligible in terms of reducing emissions. let 's be clear . in order to achieve the results you have in mind, you are in fact forced to impose ethical or green taxes, which are a real blow, which will have a very heavy impact on businesses and workers with relocations, impoverishment and wage cuts. let me give you an example. in italy, to apply this regulation in the way and at the time you envisage means introducing new taxes, new bureaucracy, sacrificing entire italian production chains, in favour of whom? - what 's that ? chinese scooters, french or german cars with chinese batteries! in short, you have in mind sustainable development - so defined - which is only for european countries, because you are completely silent with asian countries, such as india and china, with whom you continue to conclude trade agreements without saying anything, and i say nothing, about the environment! so, we are in an economic crisis, in europe more than anywhere else. what do you do? push for measures that risk amplifying this crisis. think about it! is this not the time for new taxes and new obligations and, by the way, to whom? the ones who can't move on. this is not about protecting the environment but is an environmental ideology made up by european bureaucrats.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the commission has set the member states the objective of achieving net zero greenhouse gas emissions by 2050 with the european climate law. this constraint provides for close monitoring by the commission, a pretext for genuine control over national policies. the objectives are clear, but nothing is said about the social and economic costs: we think about the inevitable loss of competitiveness of our companies compared to countries like china, india and the united states, which do not share the climate goal. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy resources are used in a sustainable manner. the energy transition requires gradual progress, sharing and substantial financial support, which our companies do not have at present. the fight against climate change must be fought by safeguarding our businesses and above all by remembering to save them from bankruptcy.

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i cannot help but quote the famous latin phrase of giovenale, quis custodiet ipsos custodes, to express my disappointment at the wirecard scandal. paradoxically, germany, which is presented to the world as a model of efficiency and rigour, is all too often the centre of huge scandals, from siemens to volkswagen, from tax credits on dividends collected twice to libor manipulation and opaque derivatives, the latter thanks to deutsche bank. this time, however, with the wirecard scandal, the trust of the entire sector of payment cards, including prepaid cards, which are widely used by the younger and economically weaker, is being undermined, just as the non-use of cash is increasingly being imposed. in italy, for example, but also in many other countries of the european union, some popular credit card circuits that relied on wirecard as a server did not work for days, creating not a few problems in full covid emergency, with the result that many users were not even able to make the purchase. the ecb has also been active in promoting the use of cash in its monetary policy, including the introduction of the euro as a reserve currency. we read in the international media that there were macroscopic conflicts of interest between bafin employees and trading on the same wirecard stock, generating discomfort and the more than legitimate suspicion that those who were supposed to control had both eyes closed. what role have auditors and rating agencies played in certifying the balance sheets of listed companies? are they distracted too? it should also be noted that wirecard also has a banking licence and is therefore subject to the strict supervision of credit institutions: was it on time like the other banks? it is therefore necessary to restore a minimum of trust and credibility to the european citizens.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, luxleaks, the panama papers, fincen, scandal after scandal, it is clear that europe and the states always find good reasons not to annoy their big banks. financial crime is a lucrative business. this is all the more scandalous in the current economic context and in the looming crisis. what has happened to european political courage? the one who was supposed to protect the taxpayers and the real economy from financial mismanagement? the fincen scandal proves the conscious involvement of the big european banks in money laundering. these unacceptable practices must not go unpunished. how can we ask citizens to follow a code of good conduct when we let big business run its course without any sanction? the fincen files should motivate and inspire ambitious reform of the european financial system. it is time for the commission to put in place rules to govern the practices of the major european banks. all illegal financial transactions which damage our economies, our example and undermine our founding values must be punished. i ask you to impose real controls and dissuasive sanctions commensurate with the abuses. let's not give banks the right to cheat any more than we gave the carbon economy the right to pollute.
madam president, financial scandals are being perpetrated one after another, with their accompanying opaque transactions, illegal immoral practices involving astronomical sums of money unimaginable to ordinary mortals, and here is another example. and i wonder what kind of society we live in when such practices are greeted with a shrug or resignation by european leaders. so obviously we have to say our anger because the fincen files reveal criminal transactions since 1999. there were guidelines, which were obviously not followed. especially since there are banks that have pawn shops on the street. deutsche bank 62% of fraudulent transactions, out of the two trillion. hsbc, which had made promises about anti-money laundering, is now up to its neck in cash. so we must not just be outraged, but we must also take action, by turning anti-money laundering directives into regulations. because it is clear that there is a failure of the national regulators. we must take action against those responsible. i think that, as the european parliament called for in 2019, we need to put a truly european regulator with real human resources above national regulators.
i have to say, madam president, another financial scandal. we're losing track of the situation. in order to put an end to this whole money laundering and taxation scandal, we need two things, despite the wave of liberalization: strong centralised controls and strong uniform regulations. the fact that money laundering transactions can take place is partly because banks decide for themselves which money transfers they consider suspicious and which they do not. in fact, banks do not even have to stop transfers that they themselves consider suspicious. the fincen files also showed that the reports of suspicion are often filed so late that law enforcement can't really do anything about it, because 166 days was the average. 166 days, and the money's gone. that is why we need strong supervisory authorities, preferably a european fbi, well-trained staff in the national banks themselves and harmonization of national anti-money laundering laws.
madam president, we are again facing a major scandal –eur 2 trillion that has gone in money laundering and the big institutions like deutsche bank are really affected. but what strikes me is that ok, we have the information, it’s a new investigation – for which we have to thank the journalists – but now we need to act and here is where we really meet a problem at the european level: we have to do much more. there are three things to mention to the commissioner that i think are extremely urgent: firstly, as many of my colleagues have said before, we need these intelligence units to fight money laundering and to fight financial crime. it’s not in the action plan, but we need it and i think that this parliament is united behind that idea. secondly, as mr sven giegold has mentioned before, when we have a serious problem at member state level of enforcing our framework against money laundering, we need infringement procedures, and it would be good, commissioner, for you to tell us when you will start doing that. finally, and i will end here, i think it is a real shame that the council has taken the cayman islands out of the tax haven list this week. this is something that our citizens cannot understand.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the fincen files show once again the failure of anti-money laundering rules in the face of the enabling conduct of banks and financial operators, who continue to profit from crime. european anti-money laundering legislation is not sufficiently implemented in too many eu countries: this is all the more serious and unacceptable at a time when the economic catastrophe caused by the pandemic is opening up huge spaces for criminal organizations to operate. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. to this end i had asked, so far in vain and in the complete indifference of all the political groups, for the establishment of a special european parliament committee to combat organised crime, terrorism and corruption, which are increasingly intertwined and are a growing threat to the rule of law in the countries of the union. after the establishment of five special committees, i hope that in the near future the political will will will be developed to give priority to this issue if we are to defend the rule of law and the real economy from the assault of criminal powers.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, in italy too, as in all of europe, the impact of the first wave of covid on long-distance travel has been more severe than in other health and professional fields, thus forcing us to make all operational protocols more rigorous. in this new phase, however, the protection of the sick and elderly in care homes will be ensured above all by the number and training of medical, nursing and paramedical staff who, today as in the past, are at the forefront of assisting the most fragile patients and their families. i would like to thank once again this group, which, with self-denial and personal risk, has kept italy and literally all the nations of europe alive at a particularly dramatic moment. among them are many young health workers full of energy and passion: they are our future, and we need to invest in them to effectively tackle this new pandemic season, avoiding the excessive workloads and stresses of the first wave. as a young mep i bring to the attention of this house the need for greater incentives and funding for the training, stabilisation and careers of these young doctors and nurses, who are often deprived of the economic and professional recognition they deserve for their irreplaceable work. in italy, the pd/5stelle government, which often supplements concrete measures with rhetoric, has called them heroes only to forget them, demonstrating myopia and inability to plan strategically. in the next three years, in fact, italy will be short of 10 000 doctors. yet the ministry of education has just suspended the competition for medical schools, thus extending the time taken to enter hospitals and penalizing doctors and patients. i am sure that the european union can make a difference in this respect. we therefore ask for your effective and practical contribution to supporting the health personnel in italian and european nursing homes.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, first of all i would like to thank the rapporteurs for their excellent work. the starting point is rightly ethical reflection from which appropriate laws must emerge. we cannot allow the ethical threshold of ai systems to fall below that of humans. artificial intelligence is going to present many challenges. one scenario to consider is its misuse by criminal and terrorist organisations. there is a risk that the software that manages the ai will be sabotaged or tampered with. it would be naive to think that cybercrime and cyberterrorism will not make a quantum leap with the evolution of artificial intelligence systems. theft and smuggling of data, hacking, infringement of industrial copyright, sabotage and hostage taking of ai systems. this is not science fiction. this has happened before. if we are to protect the patents that operate the systems, we must also make them accessible to those who conduct investigations, such as judges and police forces. in the past, companies that have encryption systems have blocked or even denied access to those investigating terrorist groups and criminal organizations. i therefore want the commission's text on the ethical aspects of ai to strike the right balance between the transparency of systems, the right to privacy of citizens, the need for investigations to combat criminal organisations and the protection of copyright.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i thank the milan office for this link. the dsa should strengthen the liability and security regime of internet service platforms and online marketplaces and consumer protection, also considering the possibility of more stringent regulation of behavioural advertising, favouring less intrusive forms and not requiring extensive traceability of the user in relation to the content he views. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the european union. the aim is to prevent monopolies and to open up the market to new players. in addition, the final text explicitly refers to the obligation to use the opt-in tool also for political advertising. this may be useful in the case of advertising products or services, but political advertising is neither a service nor a product and each member state has its own specific legislation in the field of political advertising, so this reference is unacceptable to us. another point of contention was the idea of creating a new european supervisory agency with investigative and enforcement powers. the compromise has fortunately led to the cancellation of the initial proposal, but it remains a strong concentration of the system of supervision and control at the head of the european union to the detriment of the sovereignty of the individual states, which must remain intact. i also think that the reference to e-speech, disinformation and fake news has been miscontextualised.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, let us vote on the reform of the common agricultural policy and we can make history, because nothing like agriculture can restart europe torn apart by covid, create development and jobs, improve the environment and promote our national excellence. unfortunately, parliament should dare more, and win the backing of some member states which are always conservative in the council when it comes to greening, stability, sustainability and innovation. no cuts, clear position on eco-schemes with an appropriate percentage required. we need more ambition on intensive farming and animal welfare, including organic and precision farming; accelerate digitization to reduce emissions, increase product quality and work towards an honest labeling system that promotes all this, abandoning the disastrous nutri-score and respecting made in, made in italy in particular. we need to act against the corporal in a smart way. the farmer and the worker must have the same interest in a regular agreement, with advantages for those who behave well by including social conditionality. so cut the pay to those who don't respect workers. the new cap must be a change for the better and not a perpetuation of the social and cultural barriers which restrict the take-off of our agriculture. i am therefore pleased with the extension of the planting right for vines and the inclusion of hemp in the standard markets, but i hope that the standard will reach 0.5 per cent and that all the member states, starting with italy, will be obliged by european regulations to break the cultural prejudice against this sector, which will bring enormous environmental, social, economic and cultural benefits, including against the mafias. it is a battle that must be won together, in which we will not retreat an inch.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, in these months of debate on the cap i have heard talk of a 'new cap', but if the new cap that is being put forward consists of regionalisation, of allowing member states to manage funds as they wish in the face of various social and environmental objectives, of further enriching those who already own large estates, or of standing idly by in defence of our agri-food excellence and including the nutri-score, i sincerely believe that millions of citizens and farmers would do without it. we in the five star movement call for a modern, ambitious, environmentally friendly and european-led cap. we want the funds to reach those who really live off agriculture, with internal convergence redistributing wealth to small and very small producers and external convergence based on mandatory capping and strict compliance with environmental and labour standards, precisely in order to break up the monopolies that create distortions in the internal market. i believe in a cap and in an agricultural sector which can finally really respect the basic labour standards and also the pillar of social rights. a real link between the cap and the farm2fork and biodiversity strategies is essential, but with clear, ambitious and realistic objectives to gradually abandon all forms of intensive exploitation and achieve food self-sufficiency, based on a new balance between man and the environment, for a nature to be preserved and left to future generations.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the new cap must first of all safeguard the centrality of agriculture to society and the economy. the fight for an environmental tax is a sacrosanct one, but it must strike the right balance between environmental standards and support for productivity and competitiveness. we therefore need common sense and flexibility, which we would like to see in the regulation on strategic plans, because the future national plan cannot add to the bureaucracy and cancel out good practices established by the regions, such as the managing authorities. i would remind you that there are already de facto sustainable productions, such as rice in italy, and i would stress that reducing the real meat production in europe by encouraging imports from third countries is likely to cause more damage to the environment and consumers, rather than alleviating them. the same applies to tobacco, which must be included in the list of products eligible for european cap funding. the covid crisis, ladies and gentlemen, has taught us that excessive dependence on third countries in the industrial field is a weakness that europe cannot afford. we do not make the same mistake with agriculture, we support sectors such as wine and honey and we help our farmers to get back on their feet.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, in the future agricultural policy, a part of the resources allocated will be conditional on respect for environmental sustainability, a mandatory conditionality which must not be at the expense of the productivity of our farms. it is not possible for large agricultural countries such as italy, spain, france and germany to be subject to the veto of european countries where agriculture is of less importance. the competitiveness of our farms requires greater protection of the protected designation of origin pdo and pgi. our typical products are the winning weapon, both in terms of production and in terms of economic performance, against the standard products that globalisation has tried to impose on us at all costs. there can be no future for the common agricultural policy without the protection, the financing of our products and the recognition of the work of generations on the land, which has made these products a world-renowned excellence.

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, covid-19 has taken hold across europe and the world, and the heavy economic damage already done will continue for years to come. nevertheless, the european agenda continues to prioritise the fight against climate change, restricting all measures and interventions to unrealistic targets, instead of concentrating all efforts on avoiding the most likely and imminent economic collapse of the entire union. the only instrument that has proved effective and timely so far is the ecb's extraordinary programme of purchases of sovereign bonds, without which the euro zone would have been blown up. in order to withstand the impact of the pandemic crisis, the stability pact had to be suspended. what is needed today, therefore, is not new climate constraints on the european economy, but greater flexibility for investment and maximum support for member states' spending, through a strengthening of the ecb's mandate and the removal of the prohibition in article 123 tfeu. the banking regulations on credit risk management and the classification of non-performing loans should also be revised to avoid tightening credit policies of european banks, which is harmful to households and businesses. before calling for reforms in the member states, the european union must now reform its own treaties and objectives. only then will it be credible and resilient.

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i thank commissioner gentiloni for his speeches today. in july last year we welcomed the recovery plan as a new beginning for europe, but a few months later some states are again blocking the budget negotiations, and this is serious because it marks a very strong distance from citizens and businesses. in fact, every day that passes, the billions of next generation eu are becoming less and less than what is needed to boost employment and production. strategic programmes such as horizon and the social fund risk being cut. the reception of sure yesterday was extraordinary on the financial markets, but there is still no clarity on the conditionalities. the minimum wage directive, which was due to be launched within the first 100 days, has been delayed yet again. the youth guarantee will not be the binding, universal and permanent measure on which so many young people could have counted. finally, we are still far from having decided how to proceed with the revision of the stability pact it was just a few hours ago even the european fiscal board supported its passage. we in the five star movement were instrumental in electing this commission a year ago, just as the italian government was instrumental in bringing about a decisive change of course in the european council. then we must insist on the need for early and effective action. it is time to take the plunge and do it now, because any further delay will cause enormous economic and social damage.

for the group gue/ngl. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i have been working hard on deforestation for almost five years, thanks in part to having been the first rapporteur in parliament's history on deforestation and palm oil. during that time, i have unfortunately experienced all kinds of delaying tactics from the commission and heard many fairy tales from representatives of third countries about 'everything is fine and we are doing our best'. i have to admit, i'm sick of it. for some time now, we have been witnessing the utter devastation of the world's forests and rainforests. and i'm not going to point fingers at anyone, because this is a global problem. but i consider it essential that the european union is part of it and therefore has the burden of finding solutions. it is impossible to do otherwise when we put palm oil in biofuels and european banks finance deforestation in third countries. i would like to thank the rapporteur for her work and i hope, ladies and gentlemen, that her report will no longer be altered by those who think that putting their heads in the sand will solve the problems or by those who want to blame the whole complex problem on the consumer. ursula von der leyen touched on deforestation several times in her state of the union address, and i believe that the european parliament should not be afraid of an ambitious approach and should not change the opinion of the environment committee. and that goes for you, colleagues who elected ursula von der leyen to office, please respect her words and support our text.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, from the beginning of this debate to the last speech, the world will have lost between one and two thousand football fields of forested area. in fact, global deforestation is estimated to have amounted to 1.3 million square meters from 1990 to 2016, or the equivalent of 800 football fields every hour. satellite data tells us that in the brazilian amazon, deforestation is advancing at much higher rates. we're talking about the equivalent of a football field every minute, and this trend shows no sign of abating. in the first four months of this year, the destruction of the amazon rainforest has increased by 55% compared to the previous year, but pointing the finger at bolsonaro's insane and criminal policies is not enough, the hunt for those responsible for this ecocide must start at home. the european union, through its consumption of products such as palm oil, soya, meat, rubber and wood, is responsible for 10% of global deforestation. it is time to put an end to this catastrophe, but the solution is certainly not to label products as deforestation-free and to leave the consumer with a choice, as some have suggested, but to ensure that products made by deforestation, conversion and degradation of natural ecosystems cannot be sold on the european market. this is why we need a legal framework based on due diligence and applying to all those who are involved in the supply chain of goods linked to deforestation, including european credit institutions which have had no problem in recent years in financing those who are destroying the green lung of our country. the european citizens do not want to be tarnished by this environmental crime.
the commission has already done so. ladies and gentlemen, we europeans have been working for many years to reduce our ecological footprint. of course, it is logical that we also look at the impact of our imports, especially our contribution to the loss of rainforests. that is why i very much welcome the initiative. but we must actually do something good with it, rather than just making a green fig leaf. the key to this is that the law is practical, that it is feasible and not just uncalculated risks and high bureaucratic burdens for our companies. firstly, we will continue to need and depend on imports: many fruits such as cocoa, coffee, tea and others do not grow in europe. secondly, the environmental contribution of such a supply chain law will only be positive if we continue to trade with the regions concerned and help them become more sustainable. we'll be shutting ourselves off by an unenforceable law. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. that is why i am calling for a thorough legal impact assessment and a workable scope. i am equally opposed to the creation of incalculable legal risks. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of fire is a very good one. the forestry sector in our member states, on the other hand, is a world leader in the sustainable and responsible management of forests. it is clear that different standards must be applied here. it must therefore be clear that we cannot create additional burdens for our forestry workers with this supply chain law.
mr. chairman, according to global forest watch, every six seconds, we lose an area of forest equal to a football field, or about 7,400 square feet. despite the successes in forest conservation, the data from the food and agriculture organization in this year's state of the world's forests report highlights one fact: the fight to curb forest loss is not yet over. deforestation and forest degradation negatively affect a number of the european union's global objectives in different policy areas, such as the protection of biodiversity, climate change, human rights, peace and security, good governance and the rule of law. the european union is the most attractive global market. it's time to incorporate some positive environmental legislation and goals into our trade relations with the world. we must take measures to ensure that the goods imported into the european union are the least deforested product. i therefore welcome this initiative and i trust that the european commission will consider the proposed regulatory measures that we could take to reduce deforestation. my speech is 90 seconds long. in that time, ladies and gentlemen, 15 forests the size of football fields or 148,200 square meters of forest have been cleared. we can change that by our actions and -- let's start now.
the report on deforestation deserves our full support, mr president, because we are at a turning point in our history. for example, if the deforestation of the amazon region continues at this rate, by 2030, all the co2 that was stored there for billions of years will gradually be released back into the atmosphere. this will result in a 2°c rise in temperature in latin america and a half degree rise in the rest of the world, all because of deforestation in the amazon. so we're at a tipping point. we are all victims of a global pandemic today. we know why that is. because as humans, we're getting deeper and deeper into nature. the question is: where will the next pandemic come from? from the forests of the congo, which are getting smaller and smaller because of the extraction policies? or from the primeval forests of ukraine, where today a lot of trees are cut down, for example for our ikea furniture? it's been said here, but we can't stress it enough: 10% of global deforestation is caused by what we eat and consume here. what we do here has a huge global impact. so we must take responsibility! this report is therefore extremely important because it calls for legislation which tells companies very clearly where it stands. your products are more than welcome here, but they must be produced fairly, with respect for the environment.
mr president, the debate we are having today is indeed fundamental, because every year we lose a great deal of forest, the equivalent of belgium, in an annual loss of primary tropical forests, but that is an irreparable loss because primary forests cannot be replaced by planting more trees: forests are much more than trees. so it is absolutely necessary to act quickly. the european union is responsible for about 10% of global deforestation, and we need to address safe and global standards. this is, and it's not legislation against anyone, it's legislation for. it is legislation in favour of european companies that meet environmental and human rights standards and have to compete in a market with the disadvantage of doing so with companies that do not. it is legislation in favour of consumers, who have the right to know in a transparent way the supply chain of the final products they consume. it is also in the interests of consumers to ask for clear labelling so that we know that what we eat and drink does not come from deforestation or the exploitation of forests which are badly degraded. it is therefore also in favour of those people who die every year precisely because they defend the forests. and it's also going to help us understand that every time we cut down, every time we take an acre of forest, we're taking our lives. we are all victims, but we are all responsible.
mr president, i am delighted to be speaking to you from our beautiful city of strasbourg. as the world's attention turns to the amazon rainforest, another tragedy is unfolding in the general indifference: the deforestation of africa. the forests of the african continent are disappearing faster than any other continent. the population explosion is one of the major causes of this acceleration. cutting down trees to expand agricultural areas destroys biodiversity that is essential to the sustainability of crops. the phrase "cutting off the branch you sit on" has never been truer, dramatically truer. added to this are the problems we encounter elsewhere. for example, palm oil production, which is only just beginning in cameroon or the drc. a foretold catastrophe, when we know that this is where the congo basin rainforest is located - the second largest forest in the world, tomorrow reduced to nothing? overpopulation or over-exploitation, some africans may be completely overwhelmed. we do not talk much about it, but it is an essential subject for them as well as for us.
mr president, now is indeed the moment for european leadership, in order to make sure that we don’t have deforestation or child labour in our supply chains. i would like to communicate to you a message that has been sent today by major operators in this field, namely food producers such as mars and mondelez, who say that they, as major buyers of cocoa and other agricultural products, with longstanding engagement and continued efforts for sustainable cocoa, defend the report of ms burkhardt. they warn against the amendments that are put there to water down the idea that we need mandatory measures, because that’s what they want. they need mandatory measures because they know that voluntary mechanisms don’t function. they are not able to guarantee supply chains which are free of deforestation and child labour.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the fact that deforestation is being discussed in this parliament is in itself a good thing. the problem must be tackled, not ignored, as long as it is done away with the usual environmental extremism which does not care about scientific data. we know that our planet is getting greener, as scientific studies in recent years have documented. this is the result of some policy choices made long ago, and paradoxically also as a result of global warming. secondly, we know that the severe tropical deforestation cannot be compensated by the reforestation that is taking place in other parts of the world. we must therefore pay particular attention to the consumption of forest land, just to avoid imposing additional geopolitical burdens on european companies, because that would not be fair to them, especially at a time like this.

mr president, europe is much more than the european economy. this agreement we are approving today is a good proof of that. for boris johnson or donald trump, europe was just a market to compete against. they were wrong. europe is its values and principles, against which there is no competition. without its values and principles, europe is nothing. that is why we are putting the rule of law before the market today. today we are creating a rule of law mechanism which will ensure that principles and values always take precedence over the market in this continent. it is born with the confidence of not being used, because what it seeks is not to punish anyone, but to protect us all. it is our alarm against authoritarianism; it is intended to protect us from present or future temptations by those who abuse power and it is intended that those who order what to say and what to think, what is true and what is not, do not dare to do so because they will find punishment. nationalpopulism, like all viruses, does not discriminate by border, race or gender. look, if you don't, at what is happening in the united states of america, where the most solid, stable and uninterrupted democracy in 250 years has not been able to prevent its incumbent president from not accepting an election result. if what donald trump is doing was done by the president of another american country, the us congress would already be talking about sanctions. that is what this mechanism is all about: putting democracy before economics. the rule of law is respect for equality between men and women; it is respect for diversity, for religious and educational freedom; it is respect for freedom of expression, for freedom of the press, for the independence of the judiciary. all of that is more important than the market. this rule of law mechanism is not born against anyone; it is born to defend us all. if any country goes against it, even my own, it must be sanctioned.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, a more sustainable european single market, with less consumption of resources, longer product life and greater consumer protection, is certainly a worthy and acceptable objective, but it must also take account of reality. a harsh reality, where the priority is to survive the economic and social catastrophe caused by the pandemic, in an increasingly competitive and globalized world. we therefore believe in a rational rather than an ideological transition, one which is progressive and achievable, facilitated by incentives rather than penalized by new burdens, but above all one which is compatible with the situation we are facing. the plastic tax, for example, risks blowing up entire sectors already on the verge of collapse: the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of accidents at work. there is a striking contradiction between the noble words and the policies put in place by europe: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of electronic data interchange systems. europe says it supports local production and consumers' right to transparent information, but then introduces misleading labels like nutri-score, instead of legislating on traceability, thus penalizing entire productions without really protecting the health of citizens. it also encourages short supply chains in public procurement, but does not provide regulatory certainty to entrepreneurs on the environmental requirements needed to participate in tenders. brussels is calling for more trade in the single market but then fails to invest in strategic infrastructure, such as airports, and cuts funding to those who want to expand and strengthen cargo activities. finally, europe is promoting low-impact mobility, but it should be aware that electric cars, and in general all the more environmentally friendly and sustainable products, are not within the reach of the majority of people, especially now that the fight for the indispensable is on. there are many fine abstract words about sustainability in this room, but outside there are nations in distress, where companies, workers and families are worried about the immediate future, even before the 2050 climate goals. if we really want to achieve a sustainable single market which respects the rights of citizens, we must promote it with coherent and workable initiatives, confronting not dogmas and utopias but the dramatic reality we are living today.
member of the commission. – madam president, i thank the rapporteur for her very strong words and very powerful presentation this evening, and indeed for her work on this report. let me be very clear that media freedom and pluralism are enshrined in the eu charter of fundamental rights. and i agree that democracy cannot work without a free and independent media. it’s more important than ever that journalists are able to do their job so that citizens have access to accurate information. so i really do appreciate this work and perhaps, as a former journalist, i appreciate the depth of your comments this evening. the commission as you know is currently finalising a series of initiatives that will address many of the issues in this report. the safety of journalists is a top priority that we do intend to address. we have already announced an initiative against abusive litigation targeting journalists and rights defenders in our 2021 work programme, and this will be part of the european democracy action plan which the commission will present on 2 december. as part of the first annual rule of law report, we analysed the framework for the protection of journalists in all member states, alongside the independence of audio-visual regulatory authorities, transparency of media ownership, the political independence of media, and state advertising in the media sector. the conclusion is that no member state is immune from the risks to media freedom and pluralism. this is also clear from the media pluralism monitor co-funded by the eu, with the support of the european parliament. we welcome the report’s emphasis on the importance of media pluralism, media freedom and media literacy to fight disinformation. the code of practice on disinformation for online platforms and the advertising sector is a good example of structured cooperation to ensure greater transparency and accountability. it’s also a useful framework to monitor and improve platforms’ policies on disinformation. the european democracy action plan will outline the next steps to upgrade this code. this report also highlights the impact of the covid-19 crisis on the media, especially local media, with estimated reduction in revenues of as much as 80%. to help the sector recover and support its digital transformation the commission will also present a media and audio-visual action plan on 2 december. we will also propose the digital service act by the end of the year. this aims to harmonise a clear set of due-diligence obligations for online platforms, including notice and action procedures and voluntary proactive measures for illegal content such as illegal hate speech, redress mechanisms, accountability measures and obligations to cooperate with public authorities. one of the main objectives of this instrument will be to improve the safety of users online across the european union, while improving the protection of their fundamental rights. the digital service act will be adopted along with the digital markets act, which aims to better address market deficiencies resulting from the position of large online platforms. the commission will continue working with the european parliament to strengthen media freedom and pluralism, as well as to fight hate speech and disinformation, while protecting fundamental rights. this is essential for our democracy and the rule of law. i thank you for this report.
