in writing. the covid-19 pandemic has made us aware of the importance of accessing reliable and fact-based information. in a situation where misinformation and media manipulation can cost lives, access to verified information provided by independent media is crucial. the free media and pluralism are also one of the pillars of a democratic system, playing an important role in social surveillance and raising citizens' awareness. the commission has also been asked to provide information on the implementation of the eu's new rules on the protection of journalists and the protection of the eu's financial interests. notable cases include the murder of daphne caruana galizia in malta and of jan kuciak and his fiancée martina kušnírová in slovakia for their investigative journalism. therefore, both the eu and the individual member states must act in favour of media freedom and independence. attempts to silence critical and independent media or to use public media for political gain, for example by excluding the opposition from public debate, are unacceptable. common standards and indicators on media independence at union level, as well as the collection of information and statistics on media freedom and pluralism, can help us to ensure that europeans have access to high-quality information free from manipulation or propaganda. it is also important that member states respond to any manifestations of attacks on journalists and make efforts to ensure impartial and effective investigations into such cases.
the next item is the report (a9-0197/2020) by mr carlo calenda, on behalf of the committee on industry, research and energy, on the new industrial strategy for europe (ini) 2020/2076.
the rapporteur. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, we started writing this report in the days when we were overwhelmed by the first pandemic of the modern era. an event that causes, in addition to the dramatic health consequences, a symmetrical shock on supply and demand that has no precedent since the second world war. the effort made during these months has always had as its objective a shared outcome, aimed at strengthening the role of parliament, also pending the review of the industrial policy strategy announced by the commission. i would like to thank my colleagues, the shadow rapporteurs, for this work. the 700 amendments received have become 17 compromise amendments, which are balanced and widely shared. our report divides the industrial strategy into two distinct phases: the first phase is aimed at the recovery of the production system after the emergency and the second phase aims at building a socially, economically and environmentally resilient industrial system. the need to promote and accelerate the dual digital and environmental transition is at the heart of this. the goal of climate neutrality in 2050 remains our compass. the report also takes account of the external dimension of industrial policy. in a less open and tougher world, the european union, while remaining committed to free and balanced trade, both bilaterally and multilaterally, must make more assertive use of trade defence instruments, activate measures to promote reciprocity in public procurement and monitor acquisitions of strategic companies by state-owned enterprises and companies linked to foreign governments. the report is structured around a number of key points. i will mention a few. first point: a strong focus on social aspects, in particular training, to avoid job losses and to safeguard territorial cohesion in the spirit of the 'no one left behind' principle. we know from experience over the last 30 years that transformation, if not managed properly, leads to deep divisions between winners and losers. among the latter, young people and women are reported as the most affected categories. second point: a balanced review of competition and state aid rules to protect our strategic autonomy and global competitiveness and to enable support for investment in the environment and digitalisation, preserving and completing the single market. third point: the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. fourth point: the commission has also proposed a new approach to the eu's external relations, which will be based on the principles of the treaty of amsterdam. fifthly, proposals to provide companies with the appropriate tools to contribute effectively to the reduction of co2 emissions in line with the climate law. in this connection we must avoid at all costs that environmental transformation not adequately financed and protected from environmental dumping, including through the imposition of a carbon border tax, causes relocations to countries with less stringent emission standards, with a consequent increase in pollution and loss of jobs here. the commission's analysis of ecosystems will be crucial in selecting interventions and priorities. in the report we also called for a system of key performance indicators and impact analyses, which would help to verify ex ante and ex post the effectiveness of the measures taken and the instruments put in place. the european union urgently needs an adequate industrial strategy, which is operational, well funded and properly coordinated. this report is an important opportunity for parliament to speak with one strong and united voice. we have worked with great care and passion on the text, with this very objective in mind!
on behalf of the ecr group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your kind words. this report, one might say, has unfortunately become a leftist manifesto. of course we support the values on which the european union is founded, but it is hard not to see that left-wing circles - political circles, also here in the european parliament - want to dominate their beliefs and worldview over others, bending european law and procedures to achieve their ideological goals. this is evidenced by the numerous references in the report to, for example, reproductive rights, sex education and judicial systems. the treaties, however, clearly define the scope of the union's activities and the limits of its competences. fundamental rights are not reserved for some, they apply to all and cannot be arbitrarily extended. they cannot be abused to impose a leftist narrative that promotes a specific interpretation of fundamental rights, recognizing and promoting even alleged rights that do not exist, such as the right to abortion. in a few years, we may even have the right to euthanasia or surrogacy. we need a dialogue on equal and fair terms, respecting the letter and spirit of the treaties. i am convinced that this is in the interests of all the nations that make up the european union.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the european union is founded on common values and fundamental rights which are the legal and political basis for peaceful and inclusive societies. no member state is immune from threats, but the instruments to prevent, correct and punish attacks on the rule of law, democracy and fundamental rights have unfortunately often proved inadequate. the need for an impartial monitoring and evaluation mechanism, country by country, to combat violations of the values on which the european project is based is therefore still relevant. the full enjoyment of social, political and economic rights remains a challenge for the most vulnerable groups and cannot be ignored in the fight against unemployment, gender inequalities, discrimination, poverty, intolerance, xenophobia, unequal access to goods and services, corruption, organised crime and terrorism. it is imperative that the european union place human dignity and fundamental rights at the heart of its action, so that the full development of the person contributes to strengthening democracy, freedom and the cohesion of our society.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. our common values and fundamental rights are set out in article 2 of the eu treaty: respect for human dignity, freedom, democracy, equality, the rule of law, respect for human rights, including the rights of minorities. article 3 identifies the promotion of these values as an objective of the eu. fact: in germany alone, one woman is killed by her partner or ex-partner every three days. many women are denied full access to reproductive health services. lgbti people are increasingly experiencing discrimination and violence. there are reports of brutal pushbacks at our external borders. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the future of the european union's external relations. the rule of law principles, such as the separation of powers, judicial independence and equality before the law, are being trampled underfoot in some member states. governments abuse public institutions and media for their own power. the reaction and the consequence in the council and the commission? the new annual rule of law report of the commission covers respect for fundamental rights only indirectly. the eu's accession to the european convention on human rights, even six years after the ecj ruling? it didn't work! and article 7 procedure was sent to the council. as the european parliament, we will continue to remind the commission and the council of their role. they must promote fundamental rights and take decisive action against their violation. our values and fundamental rights must not continue to decay into empty shells. the european union must be a democratic society, a democratic society, a democratic society, and a democratic society.
mr president, more than two thirds of european citizens are confronted with false information at least once a week. in times of crisis like the one we are living in, rumors, false information and truncated or unverified information proliferate at a rapid rate. this is particularly true online. they are based on citizens' doubts and fears, and they contribute to our citizens' distrust of institutions and are a real poison for our democracies. madam commissioner, this is one of the conclusions we draw in this annual report on the situation of fundamental rights in the union in 2018 and 2019. freedom to inform and to be informed is enshrined in article 11 of the european charter of fundamental rights, which this year celebrates its 20th anniversary. today, there is an urgent need to act, as the protection of journalists, especially investigative journalists, is not sufficiently ensured in all member states. let us give ourselves the means, including legislative means, to fight effectively against the creators and disseminators of false information. we must financially support free and independent media throughout europe, including local news organisations close to our citizens. the challenge, ladies and gentlemen, is to assert the right of every citizen to have access to quality information to form his own opinions.
i am not saying that we should not be concerned about the future of the community, but that we should be concerned about the future of the community.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, this report on the fundamental rights of the european union is a programme of a left-wing party, or rather, a far-left party. there are some passages that are frankly really biased and embarrassing. i will just mention a few of the many that i have been able to read. you speak of a european union which has stood by and watched the spread of racism, islamophobia, hostility and intolerance towards muslims, but you say nothing, and i say nothing, about terrorism, the victims of attacks, those who hate us europeans, our way of life, our culture and our values. in fact, you go so far as to say that counter-terrorism policies must not lead to discrimination. it's absurd and shameful! on the immigration side, you take on those who defend borders, security and national sovereignty, such as matteo salvini. you speak of the criminalization of solidarity, of unlawful expulsions, of racist policies and of human rights violations, but not a word of condemnation against human traffickers, against those who promote illegal immigration, against those ngos which systematically violate national laws, and not even against those who, through the business of welcoming, profit from migrants in order to enrich themselves. the report even includes a passage against police officers and their abuses. you say that the use of force must be limited to avoid disproportionate reactions, but you make no mention of the violence that men and women in uniform suffer every day. this is intolerable! in short, this european union is now nothing but a pathetic concentration of propaganda. then, you know, don't be surprised if the public has less and less confidence in you.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, this report is a manifesto of the so-called 'woke' ideology, which is so fashionable: a criticism of the citizens of the european union for their backward culture, for their racism, for their homophobia against lgbt people, for their islamophobia, for their lack of solidarity towards migrants. i had hoped that this report would also include a word about the right to pray in a church, or to draw a cartoon, or to teach in a school without risking being beheaded. but, as regards legal immigration, is not the right to security of european citizens, often guaranteed by those police officers who are unjustly criminalised in the report, also a fundamental right? not to mention the freedom of political initiative or thought, often suppressed on the charge of violating the rule of law. professor kleinermann is right when he says: "i fled communist romania and now i find myself fighting in the west against the same ideology". there, we're with him.
mr president, the report on respect for fundamental rights in the union is published every year. each time, its contents are quite loaded and nothing that should be of concern to us, perhaps even worrying us. in any case, it never seems to lose its usefulness as many of the fundamental rights are violated, year after year, in some of the member states of the union. the report recalls that in the last two years, reforms have been taken which are contrary to the rule of law and democracy. it is impossible to list here all the violations committed in this area in europe, but let us consider them: the violation of the independence of the judiciary and freedom of expression, the restriction of the activities of journalists, the violation of the security of lgbti people and migrants, not to mention women's rights and access to abortion, which are increasingly under threat. the report therefore aims to send a clear message to the member states, but also to the commission. the eu is not the usual suspects when it comes to being tough on fundamental rights. at this very moment, for example in france, the question of the interior minister's plans for respecting freedom of the press is clearly raised. the union is not a cartel, but it is also demanding for everyone. it must be exemplary and guarantee respect for the fundamental rights of all citizens without distinction.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we are on the threshold of 2021 and it is very sad that, even here in europe, fundamental rights are for many but not for all. we talk about freedom and justice and equality all the time, but we know that we can't guarantee them here either. even today, in europe, thousands of women and children are victims of human trafficking, sold as slaves, killed or forced into prostitution. why have not all the member states of the european union ratified the istanbul convention? perhaps because not all member states have the same desire to fight for fundamental rights and to protect women and children? we must remember that half the victims of human trafficking come from the countries of the european union. in some member states prostitution is legal and it is here that traffickers transfer a large proportion of their victims for minor checks. as long as prostitution is allowed to be considered a job, and not an exploitation or a form of violence, we would do well to stop speaking with hypocrisy and to admit that even in europe economic interests take precedence over the rights of women and children.
vice-president of the commission. – mr president, allow me, once again, to thank you for the report and to recall that all actors have a role to play to renew our commitment to fundamental rights, the rule of law and democracy, be it at national, european or international level, and that we are fully committed to play ours. the charter of fundamental rights is a symbol of our shared european identity. it reminds us that we all belong to a community of values, a community where fundamental rights are respected, a community where democracy and the rule of law prevail. these rights and values cannot be taken for granted. new fundamental rights challenges have emerged, for instance, also from the covid-19 crisis, as many of you mentioned in your speeches. as i noted, in december i will present a new charter strategy to foster awareness and effective use of the charter in the eu, and i count on your support, the support of the european parliament, to promote it and to play its unique role in the charter enforcement chain.

the rapporteur. mr president, madam commissioner, madam vice-president, ladies and gentlemen, there are elections, there is politics, as with all shows: you can choose the angle of the projector. we can decide to clarify certain facts, we can decide to leave others in the shadows and finally only talk about what suits us or, on the contrary, what we want to criticize. we have tried with my colleagues on the committee on constitutional affairs, and i would like to pay tribute to them, to leave nothing out and to have a balanced position between what we were happy about in these elections and what we were less satisfied about. i'd like to start by taking a look in the rearview mirror. where did we come from? it is 2019 and we are talking about the direct, universal election of the european parliament. we are 40 years after the creation of this parliament. we are practically at the birth of this parliamentary democracy in europe. let us all bear in mind that we are moving forward, that our democracy is progressing, and that originally this parliament was not elected by universal suffrage and was composed of representatives of the national parliaments. we have therefore reached a very important milestone and this election has reinforced this progress. the first element i would like to stress is participation. everyone was delighted with the increase in participation. we have more than 50% of the votes cast in these european elections and when i hear everywhere that europe does not directly represent, etc., i say that more than 200 million, i say 200 million european citizens, voted in the european elections. we all had, a few days or weeks ago, dark circles under our eyes, watching american channels to try to find out the outcome of the elections in the united states. we have 30 million more voters electing our parliament. let us not be modest: let us accept the fact that european democracy is strong, that it is progressing and that, yes, citizens are interested in its future, that is very important. it is also very important to note that the turnout has increased, because young people have taken this election into their own hands. and if young people have taken ownership of this election - this is the point i want to emphasise and i am obviously addressing the opponents of the european union - it is because they consider the european level to be the right lever to respond to the difficulties and crises, but also to tackle the challenges ahead. on climate issues, on security issues, on democracy and human rights issues, on social issues, the youth of europe have understood that we will only be able to solve these issues through our continental power and not simply in the national and identity folds. and that's really important. however, participation is not equal everywhere and it is indeed worth noting that in some states participation is still very low. we must improve this situation, we must continue to communicate, we must ensure that democracy progresses in a uniform way throughout the european union. but, as i said, we have good news. we have some not so good ones. as i said, some countries do not mobilise around the european elections, but we also have a whole section of the european population who cannot participate in these elections. i am talking about the weakest people, the disabled. in some countries, voting is extremely complicated. i am talking about certain communities who are not able to freely access the right to vote in certain european countries, this is unacceptable, this must change. we also have to note the lack of diversity in our parliament. of course we must make progress on this issue, there is a whole section of the european population that is not represented in our institutions, this must change. this also needs to change for diversity and gender equality. yes, there has been progress, we have gone from 37 to 41% of women in this parliament. yes, it is an improvement, but 41% is still far from the 51% or 52% we should be reaching. so that's a goal, we have to do it. we must ensure that this parliament has real, effective parity between women and men. it is also good to analyse a situation, but i would like to conclude by saying that we must also make proposals. i have spoken of the good news, but there is a bad one: the principle of the candidate at the head of the list has not, as it is, been understood by the voters. only 8% of european voters said that the question of the candidate at the top of the list was at the heart of their vote. we must therefore move forward and reflect on the capacity that parliamentary democracy can bring to bear so that european citizens actually vote for a european candidate. and here we must have the courage to debate all the issues such as the transnational lists, the single constituency, is that not so, mrs jourová, it is in your portfolio, these are issues we must debate without fear, without fear, simply because they are at the heart of european democracy. there are many other subjects, mr president, but i will stop here. we have made progress, we still have progress to make.
vice-president of the commission. – mr president, the commission welcomes this report and thanks the rapporteur, mr durand, for his efforts to present a detailed stock-taking of the last elections, as well as to outline guidance of this parliament on several key issues of high institutional and constitutional importance. we share the aspiration expressed in the report to strengthen the european dimension of the european elections and to promote broad and inclusive participation in european democracy. in june 2020, the commission published its own report on the 2019 elections. the report looked at the conduct of the last european elections and analysed measures taken to enhance transparency, to protect free and fair elections, and to promote the democratic participation of all eu citizens, to strengthen the european dimension of the elections, and to ensure resilient electoral processes. it was based on substantial input received from member states authorities, in particular from the european cooperation network on elections and the expert group on electoral matters. our findings have shown that young and first-time voters drove turnout figures to a record high, the first increase since the 1970s and the highest in 25 years. it was also an election where the institutions and member states cooperated closely and effectively to communicate with citizens. a dynamic european debate emerged on a number of topics showing progress in developing a european political dimension. in the report, the commission announced that it would continue to intensify its communication activities, to support inclusion policies and to engage with the member states in the framework of the european cooperation network on elections, on best practices in this area, including on data and reporting. the network in fact already touched on these subjects at its most recent meetings in july and september, and also discussed practices and experience in adapting elections and democracy to the post-covid environment, which was warmly welcomed by member states and which will continue. we also indicated some opportunities for european political parties to support the effort to increase turnout, in particular through communications efforts, to increase inclusive participation, and through awareness-raising about the elections, and how the eu works. in the next week, the commission will adopt the european democracy action plan. in this plan, we aim to protect our european democracies against today’s challenges and make them more resilient in order to ensure that citizens are able to participate in the democratic systems through informed decision-making free from unlawful interference and manipulation. the action plan will focus on safeguarding the integrity of our elections, on countering disinformation and on supporting free and independent media, and also build on the lessons learned from the ongoing covid-19 crisis. it will also cover active citizenship and strengthening of civil society. the international dimension of these initiatives is a thread running through the whole plan. it will announce legislative proposals to ensure greater transparency of paid political advertising, alongside non-legislative measures and further initiatives to tackle disinformation and to protect the media. i am glad that the durand report focuses also on institutional matters such as the lead candidate. with the upcoming european parliament reports and the hopefully soon to be launched conference on the future of europe, we will be able to discuss together with citizens and member states the shape of the next elections. let me repeat that i remain ready to act as an honest broker in this field where the commission’s initiative, as you know, is limited. from our side, we are ready to join the debate and to make available legal and institutional expertise, research on electoral processes and insight into interinstitutional relations if this parliament and the council deem it useful. i would also like to mention two of the files where the commission has the right of initiative. the regulation on the statute and funding of the european political parties and foundations. as announced in the work programme for the next year, we will propose a revision aiming for clearer rules, for instance on the financing. we will also propose legislation to ensure greater transparency of paid political advertising, alongside non-legislative measures, ensuring transparency and accountability, in particular online, is essential. all this with a view to adoption well in time ahead of the 2024 elections. to summarise: on behalf of the commission i can assure you that we will do our best to ensure that citizens’ confidence in our democratic process and decision-making only increases in the years to come.
on behalf of the ppe group mr president, dear pascal, thank you for your work and for the effort you have made to make us all feel comfortable in this report. the last european elections left important lessons to be learned. for the first time we've seen a significant increase in participation. contrary to what the eurosceptics say, europe matters to the citizens. however, although the moderate and pro-european parties have clearly won the elections, many citizens preferred populist and nationalist options, something we respect but should be concerned about. the system of the leading candidate has also failed because the council refused to accept as president of the commission the person who won the elections and because we failed to defend him. the general assessment of the elections is positive, european democracy is working, but there are many things that can be done better. we all agree that sooner rather than later the european union will have to face its own democratic revolution and, if it does not do so out of conviction, it will have to do so out of necessity. the world we live in today bears little resemblance to the world we knew just a decade ago, when we signed the treaty of lisbon. leaving aside the pandemic, in the year 2000, for example, few could have imagined that in the 21st century nationalist movements, populist movements, economic protectionism and political authoritarianism would resurface. just like a hundred years ago. few could have imagined that within the european union we would have to stand up again in defence of press freedom, the rule of law, the rights of women or those who are persecuted for their sexual orientation or religion. few could have imagined that something as far-fetched as brexit could ever happen. we had taken refuge in economics, we had left behind principles and values, thinking that others would defend them for us. we were wrong. when authoritarianism is growing in the world, our silence does not make us victims, but accomplices. europe is not a state and does not aspire to be one. if the policy and pressure of national governments do not prevent this, we shall next year hold a conference on the future of europe at which all the points in this report will be on the table, for example, the aspiration of europe to become a federation with a representative government and a bicameral parliament of citizens and states. there, europeans have a historic opportunity to decide collectively what we want to be and what role we want to play in the future: whether we are content to be just a market or whether we aspire to be something more valuable.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, elections are always the most delicate and important moment in the life of a democracy. since 1979, the turnout in the european elections has been steadily declining. but last year, the trend was reversed. the highest turnout in 20 years. the announced breakthrough of the eurosceptic forces did not take place. there has been a new interest in the european union. european citizens still believed in the project of integration and went to the polls convinced that their vote could be a force for change. it is now up to us to proceed on the path that has been set before us. we cannot disappoint those who still look to a united europe, a europe which believes in solidarity, in the rule of law, in the ability to face and overcome the great global challenges. our task is also to rekindle hope where it has been extinguished. progress has been made in this year and a half. the number of meps has increased since the last parliamentary term. i appreciate and we appreciate the female leadership of the commission, but the road to real equality is still too long. only 5% of meps belong to a minority, yet they constitute 10% of the european population. the vote has often been denied to prisoners and the homeless. some 800 000 disabled people were unable to vote because of problems with access to seats. so, the 2019 election report looks at the past and the present, but it looks mostly to the future. it is the aim and the task of this parliament to design the future. the citizens have asked us to rediscover the path traced by the founding fathers and mothers of the union. we have to do more and more. he remembered jean monnet: "nothing is possible without men, but nothing lasts without institutions".

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the search for peace and stability in the middle east is a difficult and delicate operation, in which we have unfortunately learned not to take anything for granted. the definition of the abraham accords is certainly a significant step in which historical misgivings are overcome and an obvious convergence of economic and political objectives is established between israel, the united arab emirates and bahrain. i think that the building of bridges and alliances should always be welcomed positively and that is also the case here. i think two points should be made: the first is that these agreements must not in any way make us lose sight of the palestinian question, the enormous plight of the palestinian refugees today and the need for the two peoples, two states solution and full recognition of a state in palestine to be taken into account. the second consideration is that agreements and peace processes are positive if they do not turn into alliances against each other and the european union cannot simply look on as events unfold in the middle east. we need a new and renewed leadership to help take difficult and never-before-seen steps in the right direction.

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, when we talk about impunity for crimes against journalists, we think of the perpetrators of murders, beatings and threats, but there are methods of silencing journalists on a daily basis in the face of general indifference. they're called reckless lawsuits, which are baseless defamation suits with exorbitant claims for damages. even if the journalist is acquitted, he still has to pay for his lawyer, who has to face the legal firms of multinational corporations or important politicians. the situation is made all the more serious by the fact that journalists are increasingly working as freelancers and have little or no legal protection. the most immediate result is that the journalist stops writing awkward investigative stories and in some cases gets paid less than ten euros gross. the conclusions of the crim special committee called on member states to decriminalise the offences of defamation and libel for those who report incidents of corruption, organised crime and money laundering. i think it is necessary to take this step and apply it to journalism, to encourage those investigations that are so useful to our democracy.

madam president, i will speak about brexit. in this process, there has always been a time deficit and since day one we have worked against the clock. this continues. today we are all with michel barnier and his team, hoping that by the end of the weekend there will be a ratifiable agreement. will it give us sufficient time to see the future partnership agreement entering into force on 1 january? i dare say, we don’t know. contingency planning should continue. a lot has been done, but of course a no-deal scenario has never been our option. so we can only hope that compromises will be reached, review clauses established, brackets gone in time for us to go into proper democratic scrutiny. the question is how far our house can go with pragmatists. we have to be clear on one issue where there is no space for pragmatists: the council decision of 25 february this year authorising the opening of these negotiations made it very explicit. it says that the negotiations of the envisaged partnership should be premised on the effective implementation of the withdrawal agreement and its three protocols. full and timely implementation of this agreement is fundamental for us here. we hope to hear again soon from vice-president maroš šefčovič on this. and we cannot forget either the famous clauses in the internal market bill and their effective disappearance. we know that in the union, political will makes miracles. but there are limits. many options can be discussed and likely will be discussed, including provisional implementation with a delayed ratification. we do not know yet either whether this will be a new exclusive competence agreement, but i sincerely hope we will not be pressed by such challenges. it is clear that the list of risks and uncertainty stays long. what must be respected, however, is this house’s right of proper democratic scrutiny. madam president! i would like to remind my colleagues that this union is, or at least should be, a union of free and equal nations built on treaties in the spirit of compromise, not dictate. the treaties tell us how we set the multiannual financial framework. there is no mention of the condition of the funds against an arbitrarily defined risk of a threat to the rule of law. the state would want to circumvent these treaties. when you talk about the rule of law, you want to violate the rule of law of the european union. poland and hungary have used their right. this parliament also blocked the agreement for many months, using its right. this is not a discussion of european values. it's not a question of european values, because we respect them. it's a debate about who has the right to interpret them. the state has the right to interpret them. we do not believe, for example, that human dignity is based on free abortion and free euthanasia. we do not think that democracy means that decisions about who is to govern in warsaw and budapest are to be made in brussels, not in this chamber.

the next item is the declarations by the council and the commission on the new strategy on gender equality for lgbt people (2020/2877 ((rsp)). members are informed that no requested speeches or blue card questions will be allowed in the debate during this part-session. i would also point out that the speakers can be reached remotely from the liaison offices of the member states.
member of the commission. – madam president, thank you for having included this debate in the plenary agenda. since the founding of the european union, its member states have intensified their efforts to build societies based on shared values of human rights, equality and non—discrimination. in response to increasing societal pressure to ensure equal rights for all, we have made some progress over the years in fighting discrimination and in advancing equality and inclusion of lgbtiq people in europe. however, we still have a long way to go. today in the eu, too many lgbtiq people cannot be themselves without fear of discrimination, exclusion or violence. in 2019, 43% of lgbtiq people felt discriminated against. in 2012 this figure was lower, at 37%. all of this takes place in a climate of growing opposition against the protection of lgbtiq rights and the global pandemic, which has compounded existing inequalities for lgbtiq people. so the european commission is taking a strong stand for european diversity by launching the first ever lgbtiq equality strategy as part of the union of equalities strategic approach. the lgbtiq equality strategy will help to build a union where people, in all their diversity, are equal, where they can be themselves without risk of discrimination, exclusion or violence. to achieve this vision, the lgbtiq strategy presents the commission’s key actions and objectives for the coming five years around four pillars: tackling discrimination, ensuring safety, building inclusive societies and leading the call for lgbtiq equality around the world. several member states do not have laws to punish hate crime and hate speech against lgbtiq people. the commission’s new strategy announces the initiative to extend the list of eu crimes to cover hate crime and hate speech, including when targeted at lgbtiq people. due to legal differences across member states, rainbow families continue to struggle to have their family ties recognised when they cross the eu’s internal borders. despite the differences in national legislation, the commission will push for mutual recognition of family relations in the eu. as the president said in her state of the union speech, when you are a parent, you are a parent everywhere. and in order to improve legal certainty for eu citizens exercising their free movement rights, the commission will review the 2009 guidelines on free movement. the reviewed guidelines will reflect the diversity of families and hence contribute to facilitating the exercise of free movement rights for all families, including, of course, rainbow families. to realise the strategy’s objectives, the commission will harness all tools that are at its disposal. targeted actions will be combined with enhanced equality mainstreaming. lgbtiq people, like all other groups, are not a homogeneous group, which is why intersectionality will be used as a cross-cutting principle throughout the implementation of the strategy. furthermore, eu actions to advance lgbtiq equality need to be matched by sustained efforts at all levels, which is why we are calling for lgbtiq equality strategy in all the 27 eu member states. so i really look forward to your debate and your impressions on this strategy. (applause)
we now continue with the discussion on the council and commission statements on the new lgbtiq equality strategy (2020/2877(rsp)).
on behalf of the s&d group. – madam president, i would like to thank the commission for this ambitious action plan concerning acceptance, recognition and protection. this long-awaited strategy is welcomed by lgbtiq civil society organisations, but also by millions of lgbti people living in europe, their parents, their children, and their families and friends. as the co-chair of the european parliament lgbtiq intergroup, i would like to thank you for the close cooperation we have had with you and also for your close cooperation with civil society. this strategy comes at a very opportune moment because in several member states of the european union, for instance poland and hungary, the governments have organised a real witch-hunt against lgbti people. this has even intensified during the pandemic because they want to divert attention from the failures in fighting the virus. we socialists and democrats appreciate that this strategy also tackles discrimination in employment by bringing forward legislation to enhance the role of equality bodies. we also welcome the fact that eu funds will strengthen lgbtiq civil society and research on gender in the member states. hate speech and hate crime is on the rise in the european union and therefore extending the list of hate crimes, including when lgbtiq people are targeted, is crucial. enhancing legal protection for rainbow families and facilitating the exercise of free movement for all families is paramount. we welcome the strong commitment of the commission that when one is a parent in one country, one is a parent in every country. however, it is regrettable that the commitment on mutual recognition of same-gender partnerships between member states is softer. by urging member states to adopt national lgbtiq action plans, the rights of trans, non-binary and intersex persons can no longer be ignored by member states. yes, they exist. they live with us. they live next to us. this strategy, together with the eu gender equality strategy and the intersectional approach, is the best answer to counter the anti-gender movement – a movement made up of ultra-hard-core conservative politicians and their followers by religious fundamentalists, who stand for an outdated patriarchal society where women’s rights and lgbti rights have no place. let me finish by recalling that being an lgbti person is not an ideology, it is not an identity, and it is not a choice – but being homo, bi, trans and inter-phobic is a choice. (applause)
on behalf of the renew group. – madam president, i would like to thank the commissioner for putting on the table the lgbti rights strategy that this house has been asking for repeatedly for many years. finally we have something on the table, and i think you can count on almost all groups in this parliament to make it even better, because we will keep pushing. in recent years, we have been talking a lot about lgbti-free zones in poland and recently also in hungary. let’s be ambitious: let’s make europe the biggest lgbti welcome zone in the world, and there will not be any white spots. i think the vast majority of european citizens are not homophobic. what the polish government is doing is not representative for the majority of people. that’s why it’s very good that this is on the table, because it’s a political statement. it is representative for the set of values that we represent, the kind of society that we represent: a society where everybody has a place, every european is welcome and safe. what’s in it on hate speech and hate crimes, legislative proposals on rainbow families, is very good, but i would like to echo what has been said by maria walsh on conversion therapies, where we want a legal ban at european level. please, commissioner, also think about lgbti sex workers, people who are very marginalised, very vulnerable; think of transgender persons, who still in five countries undergo unnecessary sterilisation, and people who, thanks to covid measures, are back in an unsafe situation. finally, commissioner, i hope that this commission is not just going to put forward new proposals but will also enforce the existing instruments, because we already have a lot of tools that we could use a lot better. thank you: you have our support.

the reporter. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, it is an ambitious objective to draw up a sound strategy for the future of european smes at this historic stage, and the text that has emerged after months of close negotiations is a solid and concrete text, which has been designed and dedicated to those who are not afraid to get their hands dirty and who struggle every day to be entrepreneurs. i am thinking of artisans, small entrepreneurs, young people who have started start-ups, employees. it is a document which also wants to challenge the commonplace which all too often sees the entrepreneur only as a polluter or a tax evader. on 10 march, the european commission issued a communication entitled "a sme strategy for a sustainable and digital europe", and the next day - i must say with a little time-wasting - the world health organization declared covid a pandemic. the aim was therefore to align the union's priorities with the changed socioeconomic environment. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we are talking about realities that are capable of employing over 100 million european citizens, generating over half of the union's gdp. and it was smes that were hit first, but they were able to respond first and they were able to turn themselves around and give a big hand to the european citizens. i would like to give a few examples: i am pleased to mention a company in my region, veneto, which has been able to reconvert and move from graphic arts to the manufacture of masks. i am very pleased to mention a german supplier to the automotive industry, which has been able to develop new air filters. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. the european small and mediumsized enterprises must of necessity regain their competitiveness, and for this we will need the help of the credit sector, also because we often speak of undercapitalised firms. the commission's proposal is to simplify the procedure, particularly as regards accessibility to european funds. as regards red tape, a survey shows that around 80% of european businesses consider the amount of administrative procedures they are subject to to be too burdensome, and this report attempts to provide an answer by setting challenging but binding targets for reducing the regulatory burden. green deal: the the environmental issue, we have to realize that sustainability has to be environmental, but also economic and social, because no one - and i said no one - has to lose their job. as regards innovation and digitalisation, we must be careful to avoid discrimination, including those sectors which objectively have difficulty innovating and are unable to do so, so that assistance and financial instruments must also be accessible to non-digital and non-innovative businesses, including those in the traditional craft sector, social services or the creative industries, which therefore need to update themselves digitally in order to remain competitive. our firms will also have to be protected from unfair pressure from the market giants and unfair competition. i would like to thank all the shadow rapporteurs who contributed to this text, a sincere thank you to my colleagues giesecke, cutajar, dlabajová, hahn, roos, pereira, corrao, a thank you to my staff and of course to the secretariat of the itre committee. despite the solid majority in committee supporting this text, there will also be some colleagues who will vote against it on wednesday on a party basis. i am sorry because beyond a text that has had an inclusive approach that has tried to synthesize the different sensitivities of the different groups, i believe that, at this historic stage, it is necessary to counter the pandemic by acting responsibly and therefore thinking about the solutions that the business world needs, therefore thinking about those who work in the day-to-day in small business realities. i would like to conclude with a wish, because i really want us to understand, especially at this stage, that those who are doing business in the time of covid are not asking for subsidies or for a little help, but on the contrary, they are asking only to be put in a position to work.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, just today the italian statistical institute announced that between summer and autumn almost 70% of companies experienced a decrease in turnover compared to the same period in 2019. there are many businesses in italy and europe that are suffering greatly from the consequences of the pandemic, which has in fact greatly aggravated an economic crisis that was already in place before covid. i would therefore like to thank my colleague, mr paolo borchia, for his work on the dossier on the relaunch of smes. this dossier, which deals with funding for digitisation and training, is certainly a very important starting point for supporting the realities that keep our country alive thanks to their proverbial entrepreneurial capacity. but europe must do more. it is not enough to talk about training and digitisation if we do not at the same time reduce the bureaucracy and taxes which are in fact preventing companies from overcoming the crisis and being competitive on the international and european internal market. so let us say once again no to green certifications and eco-taxes such as the plastic tax, which will certainly penalize our companies and at the same time open the doors of europe to chinese products with a higher environmental impact. we say no to nutri-score which is a declaration of war on our made in italy; we are saying yes to traceability, with the obligation to indicate on the label the origin of raw materials and food products, to protect our supply chains and, above all, the health of our consumers. let us say yes, finally, to the right limitations on the overpowering power of the digital giants that are killing small and mediumsized enterprises whose survival also depends on all these unresolved issues. entrepreneurs ask us to face them with pragmatism and without ideologies in order to continue to work, create employment, innovate, grow, giving new hope to italy and with italy to all of europe.
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to express my satisfaction with the results we have achieved with the rapporteur and the shadow rapporteurs on this directive. water is a fundamental resource for human life and its safety is the prerequisite for its optimal use. politics, institutions and society need to engage in constructive dialogue, and the 2012 right2water initiative is a virtuous grassroots example to follow that the european union has embraced. if this parliament shows that it is open to the contribution of all, not only does it make a qualitative leap, but it finally understands that the opposition, with its ideas, also has a fundamental role in the democratic dialectic. let 's get to the point . the control list for endocrine disruptors, pharmaceuticals and microplastics, as provided for in the directive, is a major step forward and a valuable tool. today, unfortunately, in many parts of italy and europe, drinking water is subject to a huge number of pollutants, and one case in particular, the veneto region, where pfas contamination has been one of the most serious health and environmental emergencies ever faced for years. and yet, reaching a compromise on a significant list of pollutants represents regulatory intervention where, in fact, in some cases there were no rules. finally, it is important to note that member states will be free to intervene in their own territories with stricter restrictions if they consider it necessary. in conclusion, this directive is a first step towards safer consumption and a more conscious use of water.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, we are here today to discuss european water legislation. some 20 years after the water framework directive, we can say that this legislation is still relevant, but its implementation certainly needs to be improved and accelerated, further integrating the objectives of the directive into sectoral policies, in particular in the field of agriculture, transport and energy. water is an essential good for citizens and it is not acceptable that more than half of the water bodies in the european union are still in poor condition and that the targets have not yet been met, mainly due to inadequate funding and particularly slow implementation. first, further action is needed on chemicals and pollutants in order to improve the quality of water bodies and reduce risks to human and animal health and the environment. effective action on pollutants is needed and there is growing concern about pfas, microplastics and endocrine-disrupting chemicals, pharmaceuticals, among others, starting with source control measures. more attention should also be paid to the risk of release of nutrients, chemical fertilisers, pesticides, antibiotics and other pollutants used in agriculture into drainage basins and rivers. water pollution is also a serious threat to biodiversity, with a significant negative impact on flora and fauna. finally, i believe that greater transparency on water quality is absolutely necessary to guarantee this fundamental right to european citizens.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, it is important to stress today the european commitment to continue helping farmers in 2021 and 2022, giving them legal certainty. the transitional regulation will allow beneficiaries to make a gradual transition to the programmes outlined in the new cap and will ensure compliance with the green deal, i.e. transition to organic farming, phasing out pesticides and greater attention to the environment. the protection of farmers and the environment will be central, with member states having to spend 37% of the additional funds on greening measures or on the protection of animal welfare and 55% on measures aimed at digital and social transformation. the crisis measures introduced with covid will be able to continue to be used for a further six months after the entry into force of the transitional regulation significant aid. finally, we welcome the agreement which will allow for the inclusion of aid measures for the storage and sale of olive oil. the functioning of the common market in olive oils and the olives from which they are derived should be improved and stabilised. we hope that this will be a first step towards greater protection of quality european oils and a stop to wild and poorly regulated imports, which harm olive growers and consumers.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, after months of negotiations, this regulation finally gives european farmers the legal and financial certainty they need to continue their business and invest in green. farmers will thus be able to defend the european agricultural system and work from now on to achieve those ambitious environmental goals we set ourselves with the green new deal. i believe that there are three qualifying features of this agreement: the imposition of clear environmental and social objectives linked to the allocation of additional resources; the use of the crisis reserve fund, including during the transitional period, to cushion the economic and social effects of the pandemic and special attention to the marketing of olive oil under the common organisation of the markets, which will make it possible to protect olive oil production as much as possible. this agreement is a good start to the ongoing negotiations on the new cap, which will allow europe's largest productive sector to innovate and transform, while at the same time safeguarding the centrality of the active farmer and the continuing quest for quality, health and sustainability of production for the well-being of all european citizens.

in the name of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, thank you, mr high representative, for your opening statement. we are celebrating another historic milestone for the mediterranean, a milestone of light and shadow, mr borrell. as you think of barcelona, the seat of the union for the mediterranean, i think of the treaties of rome of 1957, considered the birth certificate of the european family, and i therefore think of that red thread that unites rome and barcelona as the capitals of europe and the mediterranean. we therefore want to plan together for a more sustainable and fair future, along the paths of respect for the environment, development, culture, modernity, and we must be able to do all this by working on strengthening economic governance and attracting important private investment, but there are also shadows, mr borrell. we call for joint action to be taken simultaneously against trafficking in human beings, organised crime, terrorism and irregular immigration, both from areas of origin and transit, because the continuation of these trends will always prevent the region from progressing and will force european citizens to pay too high a price. and even more important is the human dignity that we find at the beginning and end of every single individual's life. and yet many countries in this area are not so inclined to respect human dignity in the name of state reason. this is the most important call we can make today as we celebrate barcelona, looking forward to a future where we will be together protagonists of respect, freedom and human dignity.
mr president, mr borrell, the barcelona declaration is the result of a clear vision and commitment to the future of the whole euro-mediterranean region. twenty-five years later, as you said, expectations are far from being met. we are lagging behind on some crucial issues and, with a few exceptions, the regression is dramatic. the mediterranean has become a great landmark where the hopes of thousands of human beings are being lost, and not the benchmark of peace, stability and shared prosperity that we had conceived and promised. this failure is also our responsibility, perhaps because europe today does not have the moral authority it had 25 years ago, but also because of its paradoxes. barcelona, the capital of catalonia, which gives its name to the declaration, has been the scene for the last three years of the most ferocious repression ever inflicted by a european union state on millions of citizens who simply wanted to vote. and when the european union is silent about these abuses, its voice cannot have the strength it should have in the face of the threat to freedom in the region.
mr president, mr borrell, the barcelona process was an ambitious initiative, the objectives of which have unfortunately not yet been achieved. but a lot has changed since 1995. the countries concerned are more numerous and more heterogeneous, and the situation in the mediterranean is more complex because of various factors: the economic crisis, the rise of islamist terrorism, the birth of the union for the mediterranean below expectations, the so-called arab spring, the serious situation in syria and libya, the refugee crisis and irregular migration, and finally the defiant nationalism of turkey. and while in post-brexit europe, the anachronistic populisms and radical nationalisms are on the rise, as we have just heard. but the european union must give priority to the mediterranean and its many challenges. it is essential to strengthen the dialogue with our southern neighbours and to relaunch the idea of a euro-mediterranean free trade area, which was the main economic objective of the barcelona process for the economic and social modernisation of the southern shore. i hope that the next joint communication - i think by this spring - from the commission and the european external action service will be really ambitious and take into account that stability and prosperity in the mediterranean are really essential for europe.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the 25th anniversary of the barcelona process is an opportunity to reflect on the results achieved, the great difficulties encountered and the current state of the process which sought to make the mediterranean a common area of peace, stability and prosperity through the instruments of dialogue, cooperation and social and cultural encounter. unfortunately, today, 25 years later, we have to say that instead of looking at that area - the mediterranean - and imagining it as a place to build bridges and develop, we have seen it as a source of problems that often have to be hidden like dust under the carpet and the results are unfortunately in plain sight. we must not be satisfied with this, and that is why i believe that we must start from the ambitious expectations of 25 years ago and give the mediterranean the central role it deserves. the challenges of peace, security, the fight against terrorism, respect for human rights, environmental protection and a positive and truly supportive approach to immigration are at stake.
mr president, commissioner, minister, ladies and gentlemen, i find it disconcerting to insist on telling our young people how beautiful and attractive an education and training system is, when in reality it is not, in relation to their future in work and society, because it is a system that has been sacrificed in recent years, also because of austerity. this council proposal risks becoming a dream book if we do not address some crucial issues: replace 'recommendations' with legally binding acts; the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the union's policy on the environment and climate change, and will continue to monitor the implementation of the union's policy on climate change and its impact on the environment. the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the new rules and to monitor the implementation of the new rules. recognise lifelong vocational training as a right, to address the digital and environmental transition through upskilling and reskilling. finally, and most importantly, to restore the working and social dignity of young people who undertake vocational training, starting with a genuine european reform of apprenticeship systems, because too often these young people are poorly paid and lack the minimum protection.
in the name of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i seem to be noticing a certain euphoria among the members of this house for an ambiguous agreement, arrived at after months of exhausting negotiations and political positions which have shown the fractures and contradictions between the european institutions. the majority resolution we are about to vote on today is, in my view, contemptuous in tone and dangerous in method, and it seems that for those who see this european dimension as an absolute, the body which brings together the heads of state and government of the union - the european council - which in my view is the ultimate expression of our countries, has no legitimacy whatsoever. let us not forget that the european union exists because of the will of the member states to share sovereignty and objectives and that it is the member states that give legitimacy to the union and not the other way around. i find it frankly offensive and arrogant that the council's statements are called superfluous, as in the resolution, and that it even goes so far as to threaten the european commission and the other institutions if they do not follow the dictates of this parliament. i believe that this delusion of omnipotence must be stopped and that is why - contrary to what mr cioloş said - i would like to reaffirm an indispensable principle here, namely that important decisions must be taken unanimously, because this is the only truly democratic method, in my view, which protects those who think differently, which allows european democracies to defend themselves against the political and arbitrary use of certain rules. it is useless to defend the rights of minorities with words and resolutions if we want to impose a dictatorship of the single thought and delegitimize those who do not align themselves with it. with today's vote we have not closed anything, but we are securing ourselves a future of legal and constitutional uncertainty with an ambiguous role for the european court of justice, always waiting for the promised hundreds of billions to arrive, which i fear we will not see for several months. this muscular exercise between the institutions, in my view, has no winners, but certainly losers are the citizens, our businesses and our territories who were counting on swift and concrete support, including from the union, to get out of this very serious crisis as soon as possible. instead, they will have to be satisfied with the approval of the rule of law regulation, an instrument of political blackmail which has become an absolute priority for this european majority, which is celebrating today, a victim of its own myopia and of a political and institutional overreach, for which we will be called upon to answer sooner or later.

agreed by the european parliament and the council. that is highly problematic. probably even more worrying is the commission’s role in this affair. the commission, as guardian of the treaties, must still far short of the scientific consensus which is needed (-65%). parliament has made a strong mandate of a greenhouse gas emission of -60%, which we will continue to fight for in the upcoming weeks and months because, again, the law has been made according to the normal legislative procedure. however, the target agreed by the council of a 55% reduction is not an absolute but a net target, which is extremely worrying. and yes, of course we must protect and restore land, forests and other ecosystems in our fight to reverse climate change, but this should never be compensation for lessening efforts by the biggest emitters. if the european union is to be a global leader in fighting climate change, we will need to do more than just come up with some creative calculations. it’s regrettable that the european council gave in to pressure from the usual suspects, i have to say, to endorse in its conclusions gas as a transitional fuel, which it is simply not. if we are to overcome the climate crisis, the future has to be completely fossil fuel-free. the exclusion of all fossil fuels from the just transition fund was a clear signal and the european council should have followed suit. we expect and we need more ambition to tackle the problems at hand and we need fewer double standards from the heads of state and government. i hope this will improve in the future and i count on our common normal legal proceedings in order to draw up a good climate law. as chairman of the committee on budgets and head of the delegation to the mff negotiating team, i am very pleased with the agreement we have reached. i have been able to interpret and defend the consensus in parliament with my colleagues consistently and actively. parliament has succeeded in setting its own accents and in safeguarding and even increasing the resources for future-oriented programmes on training, innovation, technological innovation and development. these are the programmes where the eu can create real added value and make a difference. but within the multiannual budget there are still some entrenched policy choices that continue to demand attention and resources. the shift towards the future could have been a lot more pronounced, in my view. the eu does seem to have a bit of a problem finding the right level of ambition on a regular basis, and that applies to climate policy as well. europe can and must lead the way towards a sustainable transition. however, the new target of 55% co2 reduction by 2030 raises a number of questions. take the impact assessment of this 55%. no serious analysis at member state and sectoral level is yet available. the impact of the covid-19 pandemic has also not been sufficiently addressed, as i believe, as has the impact on carbon leakage and smes, for example. most of the concerns i have just expressed are also very clearly reflected in the critical analysis of parliament's own research centre, eprs. i think it would be particularly useful to have a debate on this study. it should also be clear that each region has different possibilities and opportunities to reduce emissions cost-effectively. flanders, for example, is a small and densely populated region with limited open space and with important energy-intensive industrial clusters. europe must take account of this type of diversity.
