the rapporteur. madam president of the commission! mr president of the european council! first of all, i would like to stress that the debate on the budget package this year has a very different meaning than the normal debate before any financial perspective. at the moment, the debate is about the future of europe. we can say that the budget debate has opened the conference on the future of europe. we're not just talking about saving the economy, helping the people. we are talking about what the european union should look like in the next few years. we remember that we started this work two years ago, and then commissioner oettinger was showing us the financial consequences of brexit. we also calculated the financial implications of the new assignments. and then the european parliament put forward its very ambitious agenda, saying that the european union should maintain its level of ambition despite brexit, and that's why our 2018 proposal was absolutely clear and understandable. we must defend the european union, and to do that we will have to find new means. and then there were two years of very difficult discussions and a pandemic that changed everything. the pandemic has led to discussions about a completely different way of financing. that's why the budget is smaller, because let's remember that the long-term financial perspective is smaller. we can't keep this from you. this financial perspective needs to be strengthened by the reconstruction fund and we hope that - we hoped at the beginning - that this fund will compensate for certain budgetary constraints. it didn't really compensate in the proposal, and that's why parliament fought to increase the budget itself. we have succeeded after many, many difficult conversations, and as you know, we have managed to increase the budget by 16 billion for important programmes for the future of europe, including research, but also the health fund, erasmus, which is very much needed today, and border protection. that's what we did. and that's why we think it's important to think of it as a package: budget plus fund. but, again, the budget without this fund is too small. we must make this clear, and that is why we very much hope that the member states, and more specifically the national parliaments, will now ratify the decisions on own resources very quickly, because the launching of the future fund depends on this. we're hoping that after the council decision this week, this whole process will move really, really quickly. we need it because we need to look at this package as a whole. the introduction of the package as a whole also - if i may say so - requires a change in the way it is financed, and that is why we will absolutely need a new own resources package, which is something parliament has also been working on, because this loan will indeed have to be repaid, but a new system of financing the european union for the future will have to be built. it was not easy, it was not easy between the member states in the council, nor was it easy in parliament. i would like to make it clear that if during the negotiations the council representative told us that it was difficult to find agreement among the 27 governments, we said: imagine how difficult it is to find agreement in parliament, where there are members from different countries, with different political options, with different political sensitivities. we did it. we did it. parliament is united, at least almost united, and that is our great victory, and that is important for the future. i would like to begin by very warmly emphasizing the role played by the european parliament as a whole in this whole matter, and i would like to thank the entire negotiating team, which has been working for two years - it has changed, but it has been working for two years. i would like to thank the european commission very much for this constructive approach, but i also draw attention to the extremely difficult task of the german presidency. as negotiators, we have seen that this presidency is constantly agreeing everything with the other members of the council. and that's why the final compromise wasn't so easy. i would therefore like to draw attention to the role of the german presidency, if you will allow me. for us as a parliament, a new period begins. on 1 january we begin a new financial perspective. from 1 january onwards, we can guarantee stability and certainty to all those who are waiting for this money, from 1 january onwards we can start certain activities that do not require long preparations and preparation of projects. we can start working on erasmus now, we can guarantee farmers that money will flow, we can also talk about the need to start with the science programmes. we're looking forward to hearing about the rebuilding fund. we appeal once again to all those who have worked on this process: a quick decision on own resources, ratification and finally the creation of the fund is needed. it is important for all eu citizens. i'd like to thank my colleagues who negotiated with me. i would like to thank very warmly the heads of all the political groups, including my political group in particular, for their political support, because this was a political process that lasted two years. we worked, but we were successful together.
the rapporteur. madam president, madam president of the commission, mr president of the european council, i would like to begin by thanking my colleagues in the committee on constitutional affairs and the committee on budgets for their work, and also the work of parliament's negotiators. there are still obstacles to be overcome, but it is now up to the national parliaments to do their part. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the regulation to include the new member states, and to extend it to include the new member states. a commitment which i would like to recall is binding on all three institutions. i would like to highlight briefly some of the relevant aspects of this report. firstly, the agreement sets out a roadmap for the introduction of new own resources over the next seven years. this has been a longstanding demand of parliament and finally, after more than three decades, we have achieved it. secondly, we are guaranteed that these own resources will be sufficient to cover the repayment and interest of the recovery instrument, so that we minimise the burden on future generations. thirdly, greater budgetary control is ensured over the expenditure financing the recovery instrument. fourthly, cooperation between the institutions is ensured to ensure the proper involvement of parliament as budgetary authority in the management of external revenue linked to the next generation eu, as well as full transparency and visibility of all funds. fifthly, the flexibility of the instruments is improved so that they are fully capable of responding to urgent needs or unexpected crises such as the present one. sixthly, specific provisions for cooperation between the institutions during the negotiation of the budgetary framework are included for the first time. in other words, we are doing what the treaties say. and seventh, in the framework of the recovery and resilience facility, we clearly pointed out the need for an objective, fair and transparent legal framework for the selection of projects to receive funding. and i want to highlight the role that local and regional entities must play in making recovery symmetrical, not just between states, but also between regions. in short, ladies and gentlemen, we have a very good agreement. but an agreement that is not understood if it is not within the package of commitment and solidarity, that is to say the multiannual financial framework, the recovery plan, the decision on own resources and the rule of law mechanism. and i want to talk about this mechanism, i want to make two considerations. first, it is born with trust and the hope of not being used: it seeks not to punish anyone, but to protect everyone. secondly, this mechanism also serves to send a message to the world: the economy is important, but without democracy and the rule of law we are no different from russia or china. this rule of law mechanism is the european differential. president michel, president von der leyen, i would like to finish by expressing a fear. the recovery plan is to be financed with money borrowed from the european union, but spent by the member states as if it were part of their own national budget. the next generation eu is not going to be spent by the european commission, and it's not going to be controlled by the european parliament. there is a flaw in this formula: things get complicated when the person spending the money is different from the person borrowing it. the minimum we can ask is that this money be used for something other than funding national programmes without regard to european interests and priorities. we have a common plan, not 27 plans, and we should have a common objective, not 27 objectives. otherwise we will have opened the door not to the creation of the european treasury, but to the decapitalization of the european project itself. member states may have found in the recovery plan an extraordinary financing path which does not require them to raise taxes or issue national debt. we may be opening the door to an unwelcome return to intergovernmental europe. the european debt must finance european policy; the european debt must finance the european union. financing member states with european debt is to build the european project. i hope that this time we will not be mistaken, that parliament will have its proper place and the commission too, because without parliament and without the commission there is a common market, but no european union.
the rapporteur. madam president, president von der leyen, minister roth, president michel, it has been two and a half years since we had the proposal for a financial framework and the regulation on budgetary conditionality on the table, and we have barely reached an agreement in time. we have reached the limit and this delay is certainly not because of parliament, because we have been waiting for two years for the council to be able to start negotiations. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i hope that the council will take them up. but in any case, at last, and now indeed - i can hardly believe it - we have a global agreement, an agreement which i very much welcome and, from what we are hearing here, so does the majority of this parliament. it is a good thing because we have managed to negotiate crucial points which were essential conditions for parliament to be able to adopt this package. the first thing was not to consider the july council position as closed, not to consider it non-negotiable. this has enabled us to improve the budget by eur 16 billion over the next seven years. this is not just a matter of figures: it is money that will go to programmes such as health, erasmus, research, creative europe, the cooperation instrument which we managed to close yesterday, humanitarian aid and others. and this is the important thing about budgets: getting the programmes financed. the second thing is to have a self-resource calendar. i said that in a previous sitting. there are many reasons why this agreement could not go ahead without a commitment on how we are going to finance not only the european budget, but also the recovery plan. it's a matter of fiscal justice. it cannot be that the usual people are the ones who support public policies with their taxes and the usual people are also the ones who avoid contributing. it is a question of efficiency, because the introduction of certain taxes only makes sense at european level, under the umbrella of the single market. it is a question of climate policy, because those who pollute the most should pay more and thus contribute to a just transition. i do not want to forget one important point which parliament has managed to negotiate in this financial framework. finally, another historic demand of parliament: the money from the fines, or at least part of it, imposed by the european union will remain in the budget and will allow us to improve the financing of european programmes. and thirdly, a point on which we have agreed, which is essential for parliament and which, as rapporteur, gives me particular hope: the mechanism linking budgets to respect for the rule of law. much has been written and said about the agreement, the vetoes and the council statement. i would like to say one thing about the latter: the council's statement is legally irrelevant. because when we talk about laws, the only thing that matters is the regulation. and this regulation contains exactly what parliament had asked for and what we have fought so hard for. we are a community based on common values and we must defend and respect them as citizens and as member states of the union. this is not a europe to the letter, as many would like. in this europe we cannot be left with rights and not obligations. how have we managed to improve the commission's proposal? we are considering a broader definition of the scope of application. we are not just talking about the rule of law in the strict sense, but we are also talking about the rights enshrined in article 2 of the treaty on european union. we have introduced a preventive element into the mechanism, we have added a list of examples to clarify and we have even managed to include tax fraud and tax evasion among the objectives. and something this parliament has been very keen on: protecting the final beneficiaries. we wanted a very scrupulous mechanism with control and to make sure that it is the perpetrators of the violation of the rule of law who are going to be punished and not the final beneficiaries, neither students, nor researchers, nor companies, nor ngos. that was our goal. and with these modifications we have managed to give a safety net to these final beneficiaries. in short, the rule of law regulation, which we have had great difficulty in putting into effect, is a great step forward for europe. we also know that it will be adopted, because no amendments have been tabled and it will therefore be deemed to have been adopted without even having to be put to the vote. i shall not go into much more detail, especially since we have also finished another of these intense negotiations at six o'clock in the morning in the council, so we almost had to come straight here because we have yet to conclude the recovery plan, but we are on our way. these marathon negotiations are exhausting and often very frustrating. but we always have to try to stick with the best, the positive, when we get that deal. because in the end this is our task and we must be very clear about this: to negotiate, agree, build and define with regulations the europe we want to build together. so, for my part, i just want to thank the whole team, because it has been a very broad team (financial framework, own resources, rule of law regulation and all those who are now negotiating the recovery plan). of course the commission, the council and all the teams in the secretariat and in our groups, because one person cannot do this alone, nor two or three. this is joint work, and if we have achieved a good agreement and if we have achieved something historic for the european union - and i will not tire of saying that this is historic - it is because this parliament has worked as a team and we have carried out good negotiations with the german presidency and with the help of the european commission.
rapporteur. – madam president, we about to achieve something historic today. for the first time in the union’s history, we will have a functioning rule of law mechanism that does not need unanimity to work. this is a big victory for us, the co—legislators, but even more importantly it is a big and important victory for all european citizens. we started this process years ago. some of you might remember from the previous mandate when the commission published its proposal for this regulation. we in the parliament processed it fairly fast, but then we got stuck in the mud waiting for the member states to form their position. back then, many thought that the original proposal and the parliament’s mandate were too bold. some thought that this legislation would actually never even see the light of day, but we in the european parliament were vigilant. we really took the dangers posed to the rule of law seriously because we had noticed the deterioration of our values early. authoritarianism and disrespect for our basic values had already been on the rise in europe for years. considering this, it was high time for us to act. it was high time for a change of pace. however, the parliament does not of course have the means to stop this sad process alone. we need the member states to come along in this change, and a majority of them did. a majority of europeans were clearly backing their governments in this. there was scepticism along the way and i have to say that at times we were worried – some of us more than others – about some of the turns that took place. for example, the european council conclusions from july gave some room for speculation and even seemed a blow to our ambitious goals. however, this past october and november presented another turn of events. we had fruitful and serious negotiations with the german presidency, who managed to bring to the table the council’s proposal. extremely quickly, in about four weeks, we managed to reach a deal that respected the original ideas of the commission proposal as well as parliament’s mandate and, most importantly, our values enshrined in article 2 of the treaty. i want to thank wholeheartedly everyone involved in this process, my co—rapporteur, ms gardiazabal rubial, ambassador clauss from the german presidency, and our whole european parliament negotiation team, as well as commissioner hahn. i also want specifically to thank the chair of the epp group, manfred weber, for his support throughout these difficult negotiations. now we, as co—legislators, are finally about to adopt this legislation, along with other important budgetary files, here i want to emphasise the word ‘co—legislators’. the council and the parliament are the ones who have adopted this legislation. there has been a lot of a chatter about the european council conclusions. let me remind you that the european council shall not, and does not, exercise legislative functions and therefore any political declaration of the european council cannot be deemed to represent an interpretation of legislation. equally importantly, the treaty is very clear on the role of the commission. the commission is bound by the treaty to adopt the legislation and to implement it, and it will be done as of 1 january 2021. let me also thank the commission president for her extremely clear words on this. this is of the utmost importance. what actually is the rule of law that we are talking about here? it’s quite simply the cornerstone of our democratic existence in europe, so therefore it is the most important thing that we have. that’s why this step is very important for us. it will not end everything, but it will be the first step on our way. i hope we can reach a vast majority in this house for this historic law and i want to wish our independent commission and the guardian of the treaties wisdom and vigilance in its application of this law as of 1 january 2021. (applause)
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs. – madam president, the louder the victory-screaming of the hungarian government gets, the more it is actually meant to cover up the actual facts. first of all, no agreement was reopened. secondly, none of the legal texts were changed. and thirdly, and most importantly, the rule of law conditionality is going to come. viktor orbán cannot cover that up no matter how loud he actually screams. but politically the question remains, and i personally don’t think that this is something that came out just last week, but it has been there all along. is this european commission, the guardian of the treaties, ready to stand up for the rule of law and fundamental rights even when it gets difficult? will you apply a fully functional applicable regulation that after its adoption will enter into force on 1 january 2021, or is this commission afraid of the noise of the autocrats and authoritarians in this union? i can tell you – and i have to repeat myself here – the european parliament is not afraid. with hundreds of millions of eu citizens we stand up for rule of law and democracy. listen to us. listen to these citizens, not to the noise coming from the autocrats and authoritarians. and lastly, dear colleagues in the epp – and i say this as someone who has worked closely and constructively with many of you over the years – it does not need another debate however intense it is going to be about where orbán and fidesz are heading. this party is set to undermine the rule of law, fundamental rights and democracy. the facts are clear. what else has to happen for you to finally take action? the time for complacency is over. dear colleagues in the epp, act now.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to begin by making a request. monsieur charles michel, no more excuses, please hurry up. it's urgent. i have to go. we have an emergency situation. the recovery fund must be ratified, it must be brought to the economy as a matter of urgency. no more obstacles, no more blackmail. this is an urgent matter, and it is a request, and also do not look at the european union budget as an expense. the budget of the european union is an investment. the budget of the european union benefits everyone. they do away with the 'liquid contributors' and the 'liquid beneficiaries'. everyone benefits from the european union. we have a positive and historic agreement. i thank you, mrs von der leyen, and i thank you, mrs merkel, for proposing and dragging the council into this historic decision. we have a recovery fund for the first time of 750 billion euros, a multiannual financial framework of a reasonable amount, a binding road map for the decision on own resources. but we will have to pay the 750 billion euro debt and we cannot burden the citizens. the principle is simple: whoever doesn't pay must pay. we must also try to combat fraud and tax evasion, and these are resources which would be sufficient to pay for the multiannual financial framework in one year. we got a binding agreement. it is now urgent that this binding agreement on the introduction of the new own resources be respected and implemented. we will be vigilant. we will continue, as we have done for the past three years, to monitor and work towards a strong europe, a united europe, a sustainable europe in solidarity, and the multiannual financial framework and these resources must now, as i said at the beginning, be rapidly channelled into the economy. it is up to the member states to use these resources well, fairly and transparently, so that europe and all the member states are competitive and economically and territorially cohesive.
madam president, today it is the time of pride. we are at the end of the most dramatic year in the history of the european union and the pandemic is still not over. yes, people are still suffering a lot and some of them are even dying, but we have done our job, and we have to claim this and tell this with all our voice and our passion. today we can say we have finally closed up the agreement on the whole financial strategy, the recovery fund, the mff, the revision of own resources and together with the other programmes we can count almost eur 2 400 billion for fighting the crisis. we have to be proud of this european renaissance. we have to be proud of this huge mobilisation of resources never before put in place for our citizens, and we have to be proud to have strengthened the rule of law. no relativism on democratic values is acceptable. we have done our job and it is a good job. we have been united, notwithstanding our work differences. we have been determined, not withstanding our uncertainties. we have been efficient, notwithstanding what were long and sometimes incomprehensible procedures. we have been ambitious, notwithstanding our fears. i am on my first mandate. i am proud of having entered the european institutions in such a moment of change and historic commitment and for those who still do not believe in europe, who think that alone we could face dramatic challenges, let them come to brussels. for those who think that europe is only a giant of hype and bureaucracy, very far from people, let them come to brussels. for those who have to use a credit card before entering into a hospital, let them come to brussels. dear colleagues, we have done a huge and terrific job. we just have to go on.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, since its presentation, the next generation eu, according to what was claimed not only by the media, would have found its biggest beneficiary in italy in terms of both grants and loans, but it has nevertheless pointed out that the final accounts will only be ready in a few weeks, with the final text on own resources, with the criteria and the items concerning the way in which the member states will actually contribute to the budget. however, we can already say that, taking into account the financial flows already known for the period 2021-2027 for italy, in the best case scenario, the entire next generation complex will be at zero balance, and this will always be if we become aware of all the details, the conditions, the rates and the methods of application of the various instruments, among which the most important and complex is the rrf, and not as is happening with sure whose details we have been waiting for incredibly since 2 november despite an urgent question submitted by colleagues from my party to the commission. we therefore call for transparency in all the instruments put in place by the commission, because parliament and its members are the highest expression of democracy in the union.
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, have the vaccination campaigns been launched in the united kingdom, the united states, russia, china and the european union? delayed again, waiting for a green light from the european medicines agency. as with the management of the pandemic, the european union has acted badly, without coordination and with little transparency. each country acts on its own, for example, italy, because of bad government, will probably be last in the administration of vaccines for lack of needles, syringes and nurses. i would like to return to europe, and we would like clarity and seriousness. what are the deals with the drug companies? what are the terms of purchase? what's the price? who is responsible if unforeseen side effects should arise? how long will the virus cover? and how much protection do we have for our elderly? i mean, like i said, you need clarity, you need seriousness. the citizens in general cannot afford another failure of the european union.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the ema would be prepared to grant authorization for the pfizer vaccine as early as 23 december and start vaccinations immediately after christmas. the news gives us a sigh of relief, but at the same time it is urgent to reassure the public about the safety of the vaccine. in britain, where the vaccine is already in use, allergic reactions have been reported and the ema director has admitted that the effects and risks on pregnancy, breast-feeding, fertility and children are unknown. despite the manufacturers' assurances it is clear that an incomplete trial has been carried out and we would not want to be faced with unexpected events once the vaccine is injected. we therefore want to be able to give informed consent based on accurate, transparent and consistent information for optimal and voluntary vaccination coverage. we must remember that the lives of our citizens and the socioeconomic future of our society are at stake.
in writing. it is certainly a positive development that the european commission has concluded contracts with several pharmaceutical companies for the purchase and supply of coronavirus vaccine doses at european level. however, a number of unanswered questions about vaccines remain, just days before the start of the vaccination campaign. first of all , the temperature . the pfizer vaccine must be stored at -80 degrees. how will the cold chain be secured from the time the vaccine is manufactured to its administration? in addition, the administration methods are not yet clear: how many doses will be given to people? other questions concern the price of the vaccine. why do some cost 30 euros a dose, while others, like the oxford vaccine, cost as little as three euros? and on timing, why has the uk started vaccinating over a week ago, while the european union keeps giving us vague and imprecise dates on the ema approval? another concern is the liability of vaccine-related harm: why should the member states have to compensate the pharmaceutical companies in this respect? there are many, many questions for which we are still waiting for a definite answer.
in the name of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, the commission certainly has good intentions, as the communication says that we must do everything possible to protect our citizens. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. we have heard many fine words, but they have not translated into concrete deeds, and the resolution we are voting on today is a testimony to that. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. on the contrary, it is only thanks to us that the text mentions, for example, terrorism of islamic origin, which, taken together, represents, to him, the greatest threat to our security, having claimed the deaths we have mourned in recent years throughout europe, such as those in strasbourg in 2018. the security of citizens must be controls at europe's external borders to prevent events like those in nice from happening again. we must concentrate on fighting serious crime, tackling the problems at the root and in a concrete way, which parliament unfortunately does not do today, lacking in courage and intellectual honesty. we are calling for a sudden change of direction.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner schinas, we shall soon have european legislation which will reconcile the urgency of combating the dissemination of terrorist content online with the right to freedom of expression. this is a fundamental achievement for the ecr and for mr jaki, the rapporteur of the joint proposal, whom i thank. unfortunately, there is a crucial element missing from this debate and from the joint text under voting: the european union is not only a political force, but also a political force, and it is also a political force, and it is a political force, and it is a political force. i would point out that the closure of internal borders and the simultaneous, absurd pretense of keeping external borders open represents an unfair distribution of responsibilities between member states in the field of immigration, a threat to the security of our fellow citizens and a betrayal of the concept of european union.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the new olaf regulation is necessary to adapt the legal framework following the establishment of the eppo and to determine its reports. but this parliament has done more than that and i am pleased with the outcome of these negotiations, because they have managed to preserve the independence of olaf and strengthen its investigative procedures. the system for the follow-up of recommendations between olaf and the member states has been improved. the controller has been set up for olaf investigations, who will check the procedures to ensure that they are correct, and the persons under investigation have been given access to the final report, all of which is a step in the direction we want. the introduction of the possibility for olaf to control bank accounts and initiate vat checks is also good. there is still room for improvement. for example, the controller should be a more independent figure with greater powers. the procedure for accessing documents must also be simplified. the problem of the budget for eppo and olaf and the fact that olaf investigations must also be carried out by the member states is clear.
author. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, we in this parliament are saying very clearly and very firmly today that we will no longer tolerate the continuing violation of human rights in egypt and, also because of our great friendship with the egyptian people, that we cannot stand by and watch. more than 60 000 people are detained for political reasons, in a climate of systematic repression of all forms of dissent and freedom. and from here, we say enough! we believe that the international community, the european institutions and the member states must react very firmly. the european union must demand the immediate release of patrick zaki, a young citizen who went to university in bologna, in europe, who has been imprisoned without explanation for more than 300 days, and with him the release of all those who, like him, are unjustly imprisoned for their opinions, for their work, for the expression of their thoughts, for their sexual orientation. we demand justice for the murder of giulio regeni, a european citizen who was the victim of shocking, senseless violence, and support for the italian judiciary which has initiated a trial against four egyptian intelligence agents, despite their many attempts to mislead them. we here in the european parliament express our utmost solidarity with the parents of giulio regeni and i personally fully agree with their request to recall the italian ambassador. europe, in accordance with its principles and values, cannot afford to hesitate. this also concerns european governments. that is why we say that targeted sanctions must be applied against egyptian officials guilty of violence, that diplomatic relations with egypt must be thoroughly reviewed, that arms sales must be suspended and that any dangerously ambiguous attitude towards al-sisi, such as president macron's absurd, astonishing choice to award him the legion of honor, which has nothing to do with our values, must be avoided.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, china has upgraded to the 21st century the tools to carry out genocide, using biometric and surveillance technology to ethnically cleanse millions of uighurs at the speed and precision of a computer. the chinese communist party acts through machines and thinks like machines. in factories, exploiting minorities to death, with the motto of brainwashing, cleansing hearts, upholding what is right, removing what is wrong. colleagues, dna mapping, facial scans, fingerprinting and voice recording, sterilizations, brainwashing - and there's worse - 82 global companies involved in the general silence, many of them european. 20% of the world's cotton comes from uyghur forced labor. the u.s. sanctioned 24 chinese companies specializing in artificial intelligence and facial recognition, and what did we do?
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the text before us today is fully endorsed by all. it is not the first time that we have been drafting and voting resolutions against bloodthirsty regimes, against those who repress the freedom of journalists, lawyers, men and women who fight for freedom. and i ask myself and i ask you, let's think together if we would have had the courage of these men and women. i come from a family that fought fascism and i sympathize with those who fight and have fought in the past for freedom. today the news is so fast that we had already decided to work on the case of nasrin sotoudeh who we obviously hope will be released soon but we also have to deal with the story of ruhollah zam, the journalist who was killed, the story of dr. djalali who is awaiting a death sentence, which we hope will not happen. nasrin sotoudeh's case is very similar to that of nasibe semsai, a woman arrested in turkey a few weeks ago, or fariba adelkhah. women and men who have a courage that we often lack. so let's ask ourselves this too, also in order not to render useless a debate and a resolution that i hope are important and maybe help us - we talked about it with the high representative a few weeks ago - to figure out what to do about this nuclear deal, what to do about relations with the united states, which in my opinion, will not change much in their opposition to the iranian regime, because if the western front is divided, we risk not being effective in the fight against a regime that must be fought and possibly overthrown.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to dedicate my speech to mrs nasrin sotoudeh. the ayatollahs in charge of the islamic republic of iran discredit, even offend islam. in their documents they write in the name of god, the most compassionate and merciful, but their malicious and destabilizing daily action is based only on oppression. they are men of anger, contemptuous of human dignity and the wealth that comes from diversity. as the european union we must admit that it was shortsighted and even a little naive to think that the p5+1 agreement should be limited to the nuclear issue alone, we should have included freedom of thought and human rights. we think that to run a modern state with religion is insane. the failure to integrate human dignity into the action of government is murderous and we must fight the murderers and stand with the people of israel.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. their names are a warning to us europeans. their lives hang in the balance and every day could be their last, as could be the case for thousands of political opponents and members of the public held in iranian prisons. we must give a voice to those who no longer have a voice: 30,000 political prisoners massacred in 1988, 1,500 protesters killed in november 2019 and more than 1,000 people killed in the last two years. all this cannot leave us indifferent: the ayatollah regime continues to refuse to implement 67 un human rights resolutions in the country. this is not acceptable. tehran continues to deny the evidence, to disregard its commitments, to violate international treaties, not only in the field of human rights. the european union must go beyond the sanctions regime and use all diplomatic means to establish an international commission of inquiry into the 1988 massacre and put an end to this incredible terrorism.
in writing. the imprisonment of lawyer nasrin sotoudeh is the most obvious demonstration of the dictatorial and anti-freedom character of the islamic republic of iran. we wonder how long europe will remain silent in the face of iran's repeated violations of human rights. the commission has done nothing to secure the immediate release of nasrin sotoudeh and all political prisoners in iranian prisons. and let's not forget, iran does not respect women's basic rights but tramples them in the name of religion. we stand firmly with the activists who work to defend rights and who cry out to the world, the urgency of freedom for women and all the oppressed. europe has too often, out of goodwill and fear, not openly condemned the treatment of women in many islamic countries. this silence is no longer acceptable!
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i thank vice-president šefčovič and negotiator barnier, who has now had to leave the chamber. in my previous experiences, i learned many things. my father had a small business, and i learned that the customer was always right, that people who came to him to buy things should not be slapped in the face. that is partly what some of the arsonists in this house would like and have wasted many months of tough and complex negotiations, and as a member of the committee on international trade i know how complex trade negotiations are. today is 18 december and we must give thanks above all to the fact that britain has not been able to conclude a trade agreement with the united states. it would probably have replaced some of the goods that normally come from our single market to them. someone said that, there was little transparency. some political groups are totally excluded from the flow of information. i know this is normal, it happens in other trade negotiations too, but it is one of the problems that we have discussed with our friends in the committee on international trade, as you know very well, and it is a problem that applies today and will apply in the future. i do not agree with those who complain about the level of standards in britain because, if it was a country that was with us until the other day, the same european parliament that gives the ok to trade agreements with asian realities that do not respect environmental standards, workers' rights; we have not suspended turkey from the customs union with the european union, i mean, it would be hypocritical at least to raise these issues at this stage. so let's turn the page, think about european businesses and workers and try to understand the lesson that has come from brexit.
